## 1. Context

### 1.1 Code today

**Provider built-in tools run ungoverned**

- **C1 [V]** Every turn runs with `permission_mode="bypassPermissions"` (`_prepare_query_context`, `koda/services/queue_manager.py:3122`). Supervised mode only sets `max_turns = 1` (`:3123`). `"plan"` is used only for scheduled dry runs (`:3122`), blocking artifact gaps (`:3465-3467`) and child runs (`:3593-3600`). The Claude argv adds `--permission-mode` from it and carries no tool flags (`koda/services/claude_runner.py:376-378, 665-667`); dry runs are forced to plan (`:92-96`).
- **C2 [V]** Codex defaults to `CODEX_SANDBOX=danger-full-access` and `CODEX_APPROVAL_POLICY=never` (`koda/config.py:269-270`). Both are operator system settings (`koda/control_plane/manager.py:608-609`). The startup check accepts a value set (`koda/startup_validator.py:67-72`) of which only `never` exists in Codex 0.169.0, and 0.169.0 refuses `untrusted` at config load (§1.2).
  - The runner discards `permission_mode` (`koda/services/codex_runner.py:629, 899`) and uses `--sandbox read-only` only for dry runs (`:286`).
  - Resume adds `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox` (`:259-260`), is ready only in the dangerous mode (`:312-330`) and refuses dry runs (`:283-284`).
  - So child runs, artifact-gap turns and the squad planner (`koda/squads/coordinator_engine.py:375-385`) run `danger-full-access` on Codex. The memory extractor passes `dry_run=True` (`koda/memory/extractor.py:192-193`), so it runs `--sandbox read-only`, with its shell.
- **C3 [V]** Gemini maps `bypassPermissions` to `yolo` and dry runs, child runs and artifact gaps to `plan` (`koda/services/gemini_runner.py:86-96`). The system instructions and the query go on argv as one `--prompt=` value (`:117`), wrapped in `<system_instructions>`/`<user_request>` tags without escaping (`:73-83`). The workspace is both `--include-directories` (`:116`) and the process cwd (`:363, 394, 541, 551`); no trust flag is passed.
- **C4 [V] AG-02 is implemented** (6d050b5). The CLIs are pinned in `packages/provider-clis` and installed by both images (`Dockerfile:4-11, 30-33`; `Dockerfile.runtime-kernel:3-10`). `koda/services/provider_cli_contract.py` holds the contract table (`:177-335`); `validate_args` refuses any token outside it (`:392-449`); probes fail closed (`:605-704`). `CODEX_BIN` and `GEMINI_BIN` are each one executable path, resolved by `provider_auth.provider_executable()` (`koda/services/provider_auth.py:439-460`); a command line is refused (`provider_cli_contract.py:623-629`); there is no `npx` fallback (`provider_auth.py:463-475`). The Gemini contract requires attached values (`provider_cli_contract.py:140-142, 334`).
- **C5 [V]** Koda governs only `<agent_cmd>` and OpenAI-style `tool_calls`, in `_run_agent_loop` (`queue_manager.py:4494`; policy at `:4884-4893`, execution at `:5115`). Claude's `tool_use` blocks have already run when Koda sees them and are kept as telemetry (`claude_runner.py:585-595, 905-906`). Koda's own shell, git and file tools are outside the secure default and are denied unless the agent's tool policy enables them (`koda/services/execution_policy.py:497-510`; `koda/agent_contract.py:1650-1659`; probed: `is_tool_allowed_by_secure_default` is false for every `shell_*`, `git_*` and `file_*` tool in the catalog).

**The policy layer is broken in ways the opt-in would inherit**

- **C6 [V]** `execution_policy` never reaches the worker. The runtime reads only `AGENT_EXECUTION_POLICY_JSON` (`config.py:1335`; `services/execution_policy.py:201-202`). The control plane emits the tool, autonomy and resource policy keys (`manager.py:3485, 3496, 12773-12774`) and ships the spec as `AGENT_SPEC_JSON` (`manager.py:803`), but never that key. Every dashboard-authored rule is ignored at runtime and the legacy branch runs (`services/execution_policy.py:479-575`).
- **C7 [V]** The two evaluators disagree: the runtime's order is deny > allow_with_preview > require_approval > allow (`services/execution_policy.py:40, 309`), the control plane takes the first match by priority. AG-06 (in progress as M1-E4-T9, ADR-039) makes one engine.
- **C8 [V]** Approvals are broken twice.
  - **D-011.** `run_shell_command` and the CLI runner refuse unless the ambient contextvar `_execution_approved` is true (`koda/utils/approval.py:66-73`; `koda/services/shell_runner.py:27-30`; `koda/services/cli_runner.py:107-115`). The agent loop never sets it, so policy-approved shell and git always fail. `execute_tool`'s supervised-write check uses the same contextvar (`koda/services/tool_dispatcher.py:779-790`). Only human commands (`approval.py:1125-1131, 1179-1341`) and scheduled shell jobs (`koda/services/scheduled_job_runtime.py:299-305`) set it.
  - **D-012.** Pending agent approvals are an in-memory dict with an `asyncio.Event` in the worker (`approval.py:1588, 1625-1730`; the loop waits at `queue_manager.py:4949`). The dashboard broker reads that dict from the supervisor process, where it is empty (`koda/services/approval_broker.py:152-163`). Persistence restores pending operations on start (`approval.py:86-153`; D-163). Scope grants match on the resource-scope fingerprint plus session and chat (`approval.py:1371-1389`), with the scope's TTL and `max_uses` (`:1413-1414`).
- **C9 [V]** `publish_agent` snapshots the **draft** (`manager.py:12573`) and records no actor (`:12579-12613`). It has six callers besides the API route (§2.2.3). Policy writes (`put_section` `:12115`, `put_execution_policy` `:8652`, `put_model_policy` `:8583`) are not audited, and `record_audit_event` swallows failures by design (`koda/control_plane/audit.py:22-35`).
  - The voice toggle publishes the whole draft (`koda/handlers/commands.py:2447` → `koda/services/agent_settings.py:495-508` → `_publish_agent_runtime_update`, `:482-492`); so do the audio functional defaults (`commands.py:2059`; `agent_settings.py:614, 689`) and the voice default, which also writes `elevenlabs_default_voice`, `elevenlabs_default_language`, `kokoro_default_voice` and `kokoro_default_language` into the `providers` section (`commands.py:2398` → `agent_settings.py:619-695`, the writes at `:661-667`).
  - `/provider` and `/model` write only the draft model policy (`commands.py:447, 510` → `agent_settings.py:517-553, 555-578`), and each setter then forces a rebuild of the worker's runtime settings from `build_draft_snapshot` and the draft `providers` section (`agent_settings.py:232-236, 552, 578`; C17), so a draft model-policy write takes effect without any publish.
  - Every plain chat message first passes a natural-language settings parser (`koda/handlers/messages.py:29` → `koda/services/chat_settings.py:278`) that calls the same provider, model, feature-model and voice setters (`chat_settings.py:196, 224, 256, 273`).
  - The Telegram settings-menu buttons call the same setters: `callback_model` → `set_agent_general_model` (`koda/handlers/callbacks.py:262`), which then sets the session's provider model and turns `auto_model` off (`:266-267`); `callback_provider` → `set_agent_general_provider` (`:288`); `callback_feature_model_model` → `set_agent_functional_default` (`:1403`).
  - `publish_agent` first runs `publish_checks`, which validates the **draft** and raises on any error (`manager.py:12569-12572`, `8420-8423`), for example an enabled general provider whose CLI is missing (`manager.py:1193-1209`). It computes `MAX(version)+1` with no lock (`:12574-12579`); the `UNIQUE (agent_id, version)` constraint (`koda/knowledge/v2/postgres_backend.py:1362-1371`) stops only a duplicate number.
  - Three more worker paths read other agents' **draft** specs through `get_agent_spec`, which builds the draft snapshot (`manager.py:8190-8192`): the `squad_coordinator_elect` tool (`koda/services/tool_dispatcher.py:4937`), the coordinator service's `_load_agent_spec` (`koda/squads/coordinator.py:166-173`), whose eligibility comes from `tool_policy.allowed_tool_ids` (`:130-142`), and squad membership's capability hints (`koda/squads/membership.py:214`). `build_draft_snapshot` can also write `cp_agent_definitions.runtime_endpoint_json` (`manager.py:12509-12513`).
  - All of these run in the worker process: `_publish_agent_runtime_update` calls `ControlPlaneManager.publish_agent` in-process, which inserts into `cp_agent_config_versions` and updates `cp_agent_definitions` (`manager.py:12579-12607`). `get_signing_key` returns the master key (`koda/control_plane/crypto.py:551-556`).
  - `_resolve_runtime_snapshot` publishes the draft whenever the requested version's snapshot is missing, not only when no version exists (`manager.py:12633-12636`).

**Secrets and confinement**

- **C10 [V]** `runtime-kernel` mounts `koda-state` read-write (`docker-compose.yml:249`). The master key lives there at `STATE_ROOT_DIR/control_plane/.master.key` (`koda/control_plane/settings.py:52-53`), and so does `bootstrap.txt` (`koda/control_plane/bootstrap_file.py:36`). The supervisor forwards `STATE_ROOT_DIR` to workers (`koda/control_plane/supervisor.py:92`).
  - **AG-05 is implemented** (6d050b5). No process creates a key implicitly (`koda/control_plane/crypto.py:159-168`). Only the supervisor calls `ensure_master_key()` (`supervisor.py:223`), which ends in one of five outcomes: `fresh_install`, `upgrade`, `lost_key_reset`, `verified`, `rotation` (`crypto.py:89, 394-478`). A Postgres fingerprint row binds the key to the database; on upgrade the key must decrypt one of the newest stored values (`crypto.py:354-391`), and the lost-key path needs the operator file `.master.key.accept-undecryptable` naming the new key's fingerprint prefix (`crypto.py:53, 308-330`). Each outcome emits `security.master_key_fingerprint_recorded` (`crypto.py:336-341`). The previous key is read from `CONTROL_PLANE_MASTER_KEY_PREVIOUS` or its file (`crypto.py:171-179`). Disconnecting a provider removes only the CLI login files (`manager.py:1575-1580`; D-230).
  - **Every worker imports the manager, and many worker calls decrypt.** The supervisor sets `AGENT_ID` for every worker (`supervisor.py:536`, next to `_KODA_RUNTIME_BOOTSTRAPPED=1` at `:537`), so `koda/__main__.py:53-57` imports `koda/control_plane/bootstrap.py` in every worker; `bootstrap.py:9` imports the manager at module level, and `manager.py:157` imports `decrypt_secret` and `encrypt_secret`. Any `koda.control_plane` submodule does the same, because the package's `__init__` imports `bootstrap`, `manager` and `supervisor` (`koda/control_plane/__init__.py:3-5`; `[V]`: `import koda.control_plane.database` loads the manager, `crypto`, `bootstrap` and `supervisor`), although nothing in `koda/` or `tests/` uses those package-level names. The whole-package call graph of §5.1.4 (c) (revision 7) finds 133 of the manager's 457 methods reaching `decrypt_secret`, `encrypt_secret` or `get_signing_key`, and 39 edges from worker code in 17 modules: the unsupervised branch's bootstrap call (R-9), which is the check's one exemption, and 38 call sites in 16 modules. Revision 6's probe, which followed only the manager's `self.<method>` calls and two `mcp_connection_broker` functions, found 128: it could not see a call through an object built from the manager, such as the runtime-access broker below. The worker calls that reach crypto today, with the item that removes each:
    - MCP connection env: `koda/services/mcp_bootstrap.py:222, 233, 244, 268` call manager methods that decrypt through `koda/services/mcp_connection_broker.py:17, 44, 156`. MCP OAuth: `koda/services/mcp_oauth.py:683-684, 732, 779-780, 827`, which imports `crypto` itself (`:21`). (AG-17)
    - Squad peer bot tokens, read with `get_decrypted_secret_value(<peer>, "AGENT_TOKEN")` (`manager.py:12359-12374`): the squad admin check (`koda/handlers/squad_handlers.py:85`), posting as a peer's bot (`koda/squads/dispatch.py:360`), a peer bot's username (`koda/squads/mentions.py:138`). (AG-17)
    - Squad runtime-HTTP dispatch: `_dispatch_via_runtime_http` (`koda/squads/dispatch.py:453-455`, used for squad thread turns at `:579`) calls `send_dashboard_squad_message` (`manager.py:4094`), which calls `get_runtime_access` (`:4125`, and `:4167` for the carrier agent). That builds `ControlPlaneRuntimeAccessBroker(self)` (`:12957-12970`), whose `_resolve_runtime_secret` decrypts `RUNTIME_LOCAL_UI_TOKEN` from the snapshot or the global secret row (`koda/control_plane/runtime_access.py:66-79`). On an error dispatch logs a warning and falls back to the message bus (`dispatch.py:592-604`), so without the key this path would degrade silently. (AG-17)
    - The ElevenLabs key and voice catalog (`koda/utils/tts.py:79` → `manager.py:10541-10546`; `koda/handlers/commands.py:2088` → `manager.py:10714`); the image-generation key (`koda/services/generation_stubs.py:156`). (AG-17)
    - Agent-directory reads: `list_agents` (`squad_handlers.py:79, 306`; `squads/membership.py:101`; `squads/mentions.py:120`) decrypts through each agent's default-model summary (`manager.py:3736` → `:13164` → `:3262` → `_resolve_ollama_connection_inputs`, `:2714`), and `get_agent` (`koda/services/agent_welcome.py:67`) builds the draft snapshot (`:3767`); both first run `ensure_seeded` (`:1603-1613`), which can decrypt (`:3075`) and write. The worker also imports `koda/control_plane/dashboard_service.py` (`koda/services/child_runs.py:388`), whose `_agent_catalog` calls `list_agents` only to resolve agent ids (`dashboard_service.py:192-203`). (AG-17)
    - The draft snapshot: `build_draft_snapshot` calls `_merged_global_env` and `_provider_connection_env` (`manager.py:12468, 12473`), which decrypt the key of every provider in `api_key` mode (`:2911-2915` → `_provider_api_key_secret_value`, `:2598-2609`) and then drop them (`:12476-12477`). Worker callers: the runtime settings and every chat setter (`koda/services/agent_settings.py:158, 234-236, 487, 504-507, 550, 576, 612, 657-660`; AG-17b), the squad spec reads through `get_agent_spec` (`manager.py:8190-8192`; `tool_dispatcher.py:4937`, `squads/coordinator.py:170`, `squads/membership.py:214`, `squads/coordinator_engine.py:423`; AG-22a), the skills fallback, taken only when `AGENT_SPEC_JSON` is unset (`koda/skills/_runtime.py:22-39`; AG-10a), and the unsupervised start (`bootstrap.py:48-58`; R-9).
    - Once AG-19 removes the key from `runtime-kernel`, each of these raises `MasterKeyUnavailable` (`crypto.py:159-168`). The other manager uses in workers touch no secret (for example `list_mcp_tool_policies` in `tool_prompt.py`, `execution_policy.py` and `mcp_bridge.py`, and `get_workspace_runtime_root_for_agent` in `queue_manager.py`).
- **C11 [V]** Isolation is cgroups only, and only on a best-effort basis: the kernel puts each worker into a per-workspace cgroup under `/sys/fs/cgroup/koda` (`KODA_CGROUP_ROOT`, `rust/koda-runtime-kernel/src/isolation.rs:136-140, 176-181`), every step soft-fails when that root is missing or read-only (`isolation.rs:52-95`), the spawn discards the results (`agent_workers.rs:685-696`), and no code kills a cgroup. Compose gives `runtime-kernel` no privilege and no cgroup mount (`docker-compose.yml:239-261`), so its cgroup filesystem is expected to be read-only there (`[I]`: Docker mounts it read-only in an unprivileged container; AG-08's self-test records it). The kernel is that container's PID 1 (`Dockerfile.runtime-kernel:92`, no init), so every orphan in the container is reparented to it, and it kills none. Workers and CLIs run as the same uid (`botuser`, `Dockerfile.runtime-kernel:59, 88`). Kernel gRPC is unauthenticated on `0.0.0.0:50061` (`docker-compose.yml:245-246`; D-042), and the kernel also binds a UNIX socket when its target is a path (`rust/koda-runtime-kernel/src/server.rs:3223-3231`).
- **C12 [V]** Worker env and children:
  - the worker env holds the Postgres credentials and the S3 secret (`supervisor.py:66-69, 82`) and decrypted granted secrets;
  - CLI and tool child envs are allowlisted (`koda/services/provider_env.py:182-236`), but `HOME`, `USER`, `LOGNAME` and the `XDG_*` directories pass through (`:206-222`), so every CLI reads the shared HOME (C13);
  - MCP stdio servers inherit the whole worker env (`mcp_bootstrap.py:90` → `mcp_manager.py:128` → `mcp_client.py:156-162`), and so do yt-dlp, ffmpeg and ffprobe (`link_analyzer.py:128, 186`; `artifact_ingestion.py:1511, 1568`; `utils/audio.py:123, 170, 291`; `utils/video.py:37`);
  - **AG-04 is implemented** (6d050b5): the worker's first statement makes it non-dumpable with `RLIMIT_CORE=0` and refuses to start otherwise (`koda/__main__.py:9-11`; `koda/process_hygiene.py:52-77`). Python's own startup runs before that, so a same-uid process can read a starting worker's env, and can kill the worker to reopen the window (`process_hygiene.py:24-33`; `docs/security/threat-model.md:96`). Only L2 closes it (§2.5.4, R-2).
- **C13 [V]** `DEFAULT_WORK_DIR` is HOME (`config.py:201`), and in compose HOME and `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` sit on `koda-runtime` (`docker-compose.yml:29-31`), which `runtime-kernel` mounts read-write (`:250`). `SENSITIVE_DIRS` blocks every path under `/var/lib` (`config.py:775`; `tool_dispatcher.py:2389-2402`), which includes the Docker work dirs.
- **C14 [V]** The app also uses the worker's state subpaths: skill locks (`koda/skills/_package.py:1277`), the channel gateway (`koda/channels/gateway.py:133`), room photos (`koda/squads/room_photos.py:56`), TTS assets (`koda/services/kokoro_manager.py:25-33`, `supertonic_manager.py:22-30`). A one-time copy to a worker-only volume would split them.
- **C15 [V] Shared temporary space.**
  - The memory extractor runs with `work_dir="/tmp"` (`koda/memory/extractor.py:187`) on an install-wide provider chain (`:183`; `koda/memory/config.py:9`), not the agent's model policy. The squad planner runs in `DEFAULT_WORK_DIR` (`coordinator_engine.py:380`) on the coordinator's live, unpublished spec (`:419-437`, `get_agent_spec`).
  - Docker MCP env files, secrets included, are written with `tempfile.mkstemp` into the system temp dir and never deleted (`koda/services/mcp_isolation.py:329-330, 392-410`).
  - On native installs `RUNTIME_EPHEMERAL_ROOT` defaults to `<system temp>/koda-runtime/<bid>` (`config.py:207-213`), and so would every provider home under it. Compose sets the kernel root to `/tmp/koda-runtime/default/kernel` by default (`docker-compose.yml:247`).
- **C16 [V] Git.** No Koda git spawn sets `core.fsmonitor`, `core.hooksPath` or `GIT_CONFIG_*` (a search of `koda/` finds none). `git_status`, `git_diff` and `git_log` are read tools (`tool_dispatcher.py:192-194`), so they run without a grant even in supervised mode. Governed git runs through `_run_git` and `run_cli_command_detailed` (`tool_dispatcher.py:2870-2889`).
- **C17 [V] A new version restarts the worker.** The supervisor's reconcile publishes only when `desired_version` is 0 (`supervisor.py:458-461`) and builds each worker env from the published version (`:473`). The kernel terminates and respawns a worker that runs a different version (`rust/koda-runtime-kernel/src/agent_workers.rs:277-281`), sending SIGTERM and SIGKILL after `TERMINATE_GRACE` = 15 s (`:81-84`) to the worker's process group (`:476-482`), and spawns the new worker only after the old one is reaped (`:277-281, 485-486`). The provider CLIs run in their own session (`start_new_session=True`: `claude_runner.py:479, 793`; `codex_runner.py:733, 961`; `gemini_runner.py:396, 553`), so that SIGKILL does not reach them: a CLI outlives a killed worker until it exits by itself (`[V]` for the mechanism: a child started with `start_new_session=True` survives SIGKILL of its parent's process group). Nor do the CLIs keep the model's commands in their own process group: Codex 0.169.0 and Claude 2.1.325 run each command's shell as the leader of a session of its own (`[V]`, revision 7: the shell's session id is its own pid), so a command, and any process it starts with `setsid`, survives the CLI's normal exit and a SIGKILL of the CLI's process group, reparented to init, and keeps running with whatever the spawn was granted (`[V]`, §1.2; SEC's and REV's round-6 probes found the same). Governed `shell_bg` is started the same way (`koda/services/shell_tools.py:78`), its kill and its timeout signal only the shell itself (`:102, :126`), and its registry lives only in the worker's memory (`:39`), so after a worker restart no Koda path can find it; `cli_runner` children too start their own session (`cli_runner.py:127`). **Two kinds of spawn are the kernel's, not the worker's** (revision 8). Governed shell (`run_shell_command`) asks the kernel's `ExecuteCommand` to run the command (`shell_runner.py:38-47`, with `start_new_session=True`, which the kernel turns into `setpgid(0, 0)`, `rust/koda-runtime-kernel/src/processes.rs:89-97`), and that call's timeout signals only the command's process group (`commands.rs:185-192`). With runtime environments on (`RUNTIME_ENVIRONMENTS_ENABLED`, an operator system setting, `manager.py:702`; off by default, `config.py:1392`) and an environment for the task, the provider CLI is a kernel spawn too: the queue passes `runtime_task_id` (`queue_manager.py:3867, 4094`), each runner then calls `create_runtime_kernel_process` (`claude_runner.py:430-438, 776-784`; `codex_runner.py:684-692, 944-952`; `gemini_runner.py:357-365, 535-543`), and its `StartTask` call (`koda/services/runtime/kernel_subprocess.py:152-160`) makes the kernel record the process under the task and its agent and return while it runs (`server.rs:1062-1255`). Only `TerminateTask` ends such a task (`server.rs:1306-1345`). The kernel's worker termination signals only the worker's process group and touches no task (`terminate_internal`, `agent_workers.rs:455-519`, which `terminate_agent_worker` also uses, `server.rs:3193-3207`), nor does its reaper (`agent_workers.rs:542-559`). So a CLI the kernel started, with every command its model runs, keeps running after the worker that asked for it is killed, while the new worker replays the task; once spawns are confined, it would keep that turn's grants. No startup sweep removes leftovers under `RUNTIME_EPHEMERAL_ROOT` (the runtime controller removes one task's directory on request, `koda/services/runtime/controller.py:272-277`); in compose it is the persistent `koda-runtime` volume (`docker-compose.yml:57, 75, 250`). The worker's shutdown waits up to 30 s for running queue workers (`queue_manager.py:9424-9428`), so the kernel's grace ends first. Queued and running tasks are persisted and re-queued at the next start (`recover_pending_tasks`, `queue_manager.py:8929`), each restart counting one recovery: a task recovered `QUEUE_MAX_RECOVERY_ATTEMPTS` times (3, `config.py:1374`) goes to the DLQ instead (`queue_manager.py:8955-8975`). Pending approvals are restored from their JSON files at start (`approval.py:86-153`; D-163). Bootstrap publishes only in a worker started without the supervisor, which sets `_KODA_RUNTIME_BOOTSTRAPPED` (`supervisor.py:537`; `bootstrap.py:28, 53-56`; `koda/__main__.py:53-57`), although every worker imports the module (C10); that unsupervised path also builds the runtime snapshot in the worker (`bootstrap.py:58`), which decrypts provider keys (`manager.py:12712` → `_provider_api_key_secret_value`, `:2598-2609`). The worker's settings cache lives 5 s (`agent_settings.py:41`). Turn-time reads (`queue_manager.py:2845, 3716, 4232`) take the published `AGENT_SPEC_JSON` when it is set (`agent_settings.py:436-440`, built by `_inline_agent_runtime_settings`, `:93-144`), but every settings command, menu and setter, and a plain message with a settings verb, forces a rebuild from the draft (`force_refresh=True`: for example `commands.py:548`, `callbacks.py:388`, `chat_settings.py:284-293`, `agent_settings.py:496-690`; the rebuild is `_effective_agent_runtime_settings`, `:232`). The next 5 s of turns read that rebuild, and `sync_user_data_with_runtime_settings` copies it into the session (`queue_manager.py:2845`). So a dashboard draft edit of the model policy or the `providers` section (`api.py:5637`) reaches the running worker the next time someone in the chat opens a settings command or menu. (Revision 5 said "within 5 s"; that holds only after such a refresh.)
- **C18 [V] Concurrent spawns of one agent.** A worker runs up to 10 tasks at once (`queue_manager.py:1436`, `config.py:1389`), 3 per user (`queue_manager.py:1455-1456`, `config.py:1369`), with no per-session or per-agent spawn lock; the runners hold only `_CAPABILITY_LOCK` for their capability probe (`codex_runner.py:62`, `gemini_runner.py:42`, `claude_runner.py:49`). The per-task system prompt carries the user's recalled memory context (`queue_manager.py:3180-3196`); today Codex receives it per process on argv (`codex_runner.py:171-174`). The pinned CLIs also write state into their home on every run: Codex 0.169.0 writes `state_5.sqlite`, `memories_1.sqlite`, `goals_1.sqlite`, `queue_1.sqlite`, `logs_2.sqlite` and `installation_id` into `CODEX_HOME` even with `--ephemeral`, and Claude writes `.claude.json`, `backups/` and `plugins/store/` next to its transcripts (`[V]`, files listed after the stub runs of §1.2).

| Claude `--permission-mode` choices: `acceptEdits`, `auto`, `bypassPermissions`, `manual`, `dontAsk`, `plan`. `manual` is an alias of `default`. `default` prompts for dangerous operations, `acceptEdits` auto-accepts edits, `plan` executes no tools, `dontAsk` denies anything not pre-approved, `auto` lets a model classifier approve. | Help fixture lines 151-154; strings of the pinned binary. |

It does **not** stop CLAUDE.md. `--safe-mode` disables CLAUDE.md, skills, installed plugins, hooks, MCP servers, custom commands and agents; auth, model selection, built-in tools and permissions work normally. | Help fixture lines 195-209, 213-223. |

| Codex `approval_policy` parses as `untrusted`, `on-failure`, `on-request`, `granular`, `never`; `untrusted` fails config load. | `codex -c approval_policy="<v>" features list` for each value (revision 2). |
| Codex config keys exist with these types: `sandbox_mode`, `sandbox_workspace_write.network_access` (bool), `sandbox_workspace_write.exclude_slash_tmp` (bool), `sandbox_workspace_write.exclude_tmpdir_env_var` (bool), `sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots` (sequence), `shell_environment_policy.*`, `mcp_servers`, `web_search`, `project_doc_max_bytes`, `agents.enabled` (bool), `skills.include_instructions` (bool), `skills.bundled.enabled` (bool), `features.<name>` (bool). `include_apply_patch_tool` and `tools.view_image` are unknown and silently ignored. | `codex -c <key>=<wrong type> features list`: each typed key errors naming itself; unknown keys are accepted silently. |
| **Codex's inner sandboxes cannot start inside a Landlock domain.** `codex sandbox -c sandbox_mode="read-only"` and `"workspace-write"` work unconfined and after `no_new_privs` alone, and fail with `bwrap: Failed to make / slave: Operation not permitted` inside an allow-all Landlock ruleset. `--enable use_legacy_landlock` panics (`linux_run_main.rs:391`). `danger-full-access` runs inside the same ruleset. Codex itself (`codex debug prompt-input`) runs inside it. | `codex sandbox -c sandbox_mode=<m> -- /bin/sh -c <probe>` unconfined, after `prctl(PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS)`, and after the Landlock helper; kernel 6.18, ABI 7. |
| Unconfined, `read-only` refuses writes ("Read-only file system") and sockets (EPERM) but **reads every file the uid can read**; the sandboxed shell has its own pid namespace. `workspace-write` writes the cwd, `/tmp`, `$TMPDIR` and anything under them (it wrote `$CODEX_HOME/AGENTS.md` when that home was under `/tmp`). With `exclude_slash_tmp=true`, `exclude_tmpdir_env_var=true` and `writable_roots=["<task tmp>"]` only the cwd and the task tmp are writable; sockets stay EPERM unless `network_access=true`. | `codex sandbox` probes that cat a file outside the cwd, write each path, and create a socket. |
| In Codex 0.169.0, 72 features report `true` by default (revision 3 said 71; it missed `instant_interrupt`, "under development", `true`), among them `browser_use`, `browser_use_external`, `browser_use_full_cdp_access`, `computer_use`, `in_app_browser`, `in_app_local_automation`, `multi_agent`, `image_generation`, `guardian_approval`, `daemon_auto_start`, `remote_plugin`, `skill_mcp_dependency_install`, `workspace_dependencies`, `tool_suggest`, `shell_snapshot`, `view_image`. An unknown feature name is an error. `--disable unified_exec` leaves it reported `true`; `--disable shell_tool` removes the shell tools. Some removed features stay `true` (`item_ids`, `tui_app_server`, `worktrees`). With the 32 `--disable` flags of §2.3.3, `-c agents.enabled=false` and the skills keys, 40 stay `true`; with `--disable shell_tool` as well, 39. Disabling `instant_interrupt` leaves the `gpt-6-sol` prompt input byte-for-byte the same apart from message ids and timestamps. | `codex features list`; `codex --disable <f> features list` per feature; `codex debug prompt-input` with and without `--disable instant_interrupt`. |
| `codex debug prompt-input` renders tools offline only for models whose catalog entry is `tool_mode: code_mode_only`: `gpt-6-*`, `gpt-6.1-*`, `gpt-5.6-*` and the `gpt-daybreak-*` models. For every model Koda configures (`CODEX_AVAILABLE_MODELS`: `gpt-5.5`, `gpt-5.4`, `gpt-5.4-mini`, `gpt-5.4-nano`, `gpt-5.3-codex`, `gpt-5.3-codex-spark`, `gpt-5.2`, `koda/config.py:278-281`; default `gpt-5.5`, `:282-287`) it renders no tool list, with or without `--disable shell_tool` (2596 to 2599 bytes). `gpt-5.5` is in the catalog with no `tool_mode`; the others are not in it. | `codex debug models`; `codex debug prompt-input -c model="<m>" … hi` for each configured model. |
| **The tools Codex sends are visible offline in its first request.** `codex exec` with `-c model_provider="kodastub"`, `-c model_providers.kodastub.base_url="http://127.0.0.1:<port>/v1"`, `wire_api="responses"`, `env_key` naming a variable that holds a dummy key, and retries set to 0, posts one `/v1/responses` request to a local stub; the stub records it and answers 400, and nothing else leaves the host. At `none` (the §2.3.3 every-level flags, `--disable shell_tool`, `--sandbox read-only`), the request's `tools` are `apply_patch` and `request_user_input` for `gpt-5.5`, and `request_user_input` alone for the other six configured models. With the shell enabled, `exec_command` and `write_stdin` are added for every one of them. Code-mode models send no `tools` array, so they are `codex_none_unproven` (§2.3.3). | The stub capture, per configured model, at `none` and with the shell. |
| **Codex refuses every forced call at `none`.** The Responses stub answers the first request with one forced call, and the follow-up request records Codex's answer. At `none` (the §2.3.3 every-level flags, `--disable shell_tool`, `--sandbox read-only`), for `gpt-5.5` and `gpt-5.4`: `exec_command`, `write_stdin`, `shell`, `shell_command` and `view_image` return `unsupported call: <name>`; a `local_shell_call` item is ignored (no follow-up request); `apply_patch` as a custom tool call, adding a file inside or outside the workspace, returns `patch rejected: writing is blocked by read-only sandbox; rejected by user approval settings` on `gpt-5.5` and `unsupported custom tool call: apply_patch` on `gpt-5.4`; `apply_patch` as a function call returns `aborted` on `gpt-5.5` and `unsupported call: apply_patch` on `gpt-5.4`. No file is created and the outside canary never reaches a request. For `gpt-5.4-mini`, `gpt-5.4-nano`, `gpt-5.3-codex`, `gpt-5.3-codex-spark` and `gpt-5.2`, the forced `exec_command` and `apply_patch` are refused the same way. The follow-up request carries the same tool list as the first. Positive control with `--sandbox danger-full-access` and the shell: the forced `exec_command` runs and returns the outside canary on `gpt-5.5` and `gpt-5.4`, and `apply_patch` writes inside and outside the workspace on `gpt-5.5`; `shell`, `shell_command` and `local_shell_call` stay refused at that level too. | The Responses stub; one run per model, forced call and level. |
| **Codex reads its developer instructions from `CODEX_HOME`.** With `developer_instructions = "KODA_DEV_CANARY_A"` in `$CODEX_HOME/config.toml`, `codex debug prompt-input -c model="gpt-5.5" hi` contains `KODA_DEV_CANARY_A`, and a second home holding `…_B` shows only `…_B`. | Two homes, one prompt-input run each. |
| **Claude resumes from a transcript copied into a fresh home.** Turn 1 runs in home A against the Anthropic stub. Copying only `projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl` into an empty home B and running `claude -p --resume <session>` there sends a request that holds turn 1's prompt and reply and the new prompt, under the same session id, and home B's copy of that file grows. | Claude 2.1.325 (the platform binary) with the §2.3.1 every-level flags, `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` at the stub and a dummy `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`. |
| **Concurrent spawns of one agent see only their own configuration (revision 6).** Two `codex exec` runs of one agent start at once against one Responses stub, each with its own home (`provider-homes/agent1/spawns/<incarnation>/<A\|B>`) whose `config.toml` holds `developer_instructions` with its own canary and memory-context canary: A at `none` (`--disable shell_tool --sandbox read-only`), B with the shell (`--sandbox danger-full-access`), both `gpt-5.5`. A's request carries only A's two canaries and the tools `apply_patch`, `request_user_input`; B's carries only B's and adds `exec_command`, `write_stdin`. Two Gemini runs at once, A at `none` and B at `read_only`, each with its own home, settings and cwd: A's request has no tools and no workspace canary, B's has the four read tools and the workspace-root canary, and each names only its own cwd. | `codex exec` and `gemini` with the §2.3.3 and §2.3.2 flags, two processes started together, one loopback stub each for Codex and Gemini, inside `unshare -rn`. |

A symbolic link planted at the target path makes that create fail (`EEXIST`), so a pre-planted link is never written through. | Claude 2.1.325 (the platform binary) with the §2.3.1 every-level flags at the Anthropic stub; a Python `os.open` with those flags. |

| **Model-started processes outlive the CLI unless the spawn's root kills them (revision 7).** Codex 0.169.0 at `workspace_exec` (the §2.3.3 flags, `danger-full-access`) and Claude 2.1.325 (the platform binary, the §2.3.1 flags with `--tools …,Bash`), each against its stub, with a forced command that starts a `setsid` background process and, in the kill cases, a 9 s foreground command; each writes a file in the workspace when it finishes. The "worker" starts the helper in its own session and keeps the only write end of the lifeline pipe; the helper applies the Landlock ruleset in the CLI's process only. With no mechanism (the helper kills only the CLI, as today's runners do), the background process outlives the CLI's normal exit, and both commands outlive a SIGKILL of the worker's process group, reparented to init, and write their files. With `pid_namespace` (an identity-mapped user and PID namespace whose init is the helper's first child, with `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` towards the helper; this prototype kept the host's `/proc`, which revision 8 replaces), `cgroup` (the CLI's process joins a new cgroup v2 before Landlock, and the end writes `cgroup.kill`) or `subreaper` (`PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER`, then SIGKILL of every child until none is left), no process remains and no file is written after the CLI's normal exit or after a SIGKILL of the worker's process group (the lifeline closes), for both CLIs. After a SIGKILL of the helper itself, none remains with `pid_namespace` (init's death signal ends the namespace) or with `cgroup` (the worker sees the helper's signal exit and writes `cgroup.kill`); with `subreaper` the CLI and both commands survive and write their files (R-10). Both CLIs complete their turn in every mode. As uid 65534, the identity map works and a `setsid` grandchild is gone at the child's exit. Codex's inner `workspace-write` sandbox gives the same result inside the namespace with no ruleset as without one (a workspace write passes; an outside write and a socket are refused). | Probe scripts with the Responses and Anthropic stubs, inside `unshare -rn`; kernel 6.18 with cgroup2 mounted read-write on this host. |
| **A PID namespace needs its own `/proc` (revision 8).** A probe started inside an identity-mapped user and PID namespace, under a Landlock ruleset (ABI 7) that grants `/proc` read-only, with the host's `/proc`: `getpid()` is 2 but `/proc/self` names the outer pid, `/proc/2/comm` reads `kthreadd`, the host's processes are listed (the outer helper among them), `pgrep -P <shell>` finds none of the shell's children, and `pkill` returns 1 with its target alive. With init in a new mount namespace that made `/` recursively private and mounted a fresh procfs before the command applied the same ruleset: `/proc/self` is the process's own pid, `/proc/<getpid()>/comm` its own name, only pids 1 and 2 are listed (the outer helper is not), `pgrep -P <shell>` finds the shell's `sleep` and `kill` ends it, and `pkill` returns 0 and ends its target. | A Python probe with a Landlock helper, run in both modes; kernel 6.18. |

| **A spawn cgroup can be a child of a populated workspace cgroup (revision 8).** On this host's cgroup2 hierarchy, with no controller enabled in either `cgroup.subtree_control`, a stand-in worker process sits in `ws_a/`, and a helper started there reads its own cgroup from `/proc/self/cgroup`, creates `ws_a/spawn_x/` and starts a command in it that leaves a `setsid` grandchild. Writing `1` to `spawn_x/cgroup.kill` ends the command and the grandchild (`cgroup.events` reads `populated 0`; no canary), `spawn_x` is removed, and the process in `ws_a` keeps running. With a helper that is not a subreaper (revision 8's `cgroup` mode), the orphan is reparented to the kernel stand-in and killed by it within 1.5 s, while the spawn still runs. | **Startup context at every level (corrects revision 3 row 7).** When the cwd is trusted, `discoverMemoryPaths` loads, for **every workspace directory** (the cwd and each `--include-directories` entry), the GEMINI.md files from that directory up to the nearest ancestor holding a boundary marker (default `.git`), or that directory alone when no ancestor has one. `context.loadMemoryFromIncludeDirectories` only controls `/memory reload`. `context.includeDirectoryTree` (default `true`) adds each workspace directory's tree to the first request. `context.memoryBoundaryMarkers=[]` disables the climb: the ceiling is then the directory itself, for startup and JIT alike. The same setting bounds `@` imports: an import resolves only inside the importing file's own directory, checked on real paths, so an absolute, relative or symlinked import of a file outside it is dropped. The GEMINI.md file itself is read with `fs.readFile`, so a GEMINI.md that is a symlink is followed, at startup and in JIT discovery; the file tools instead check the real path against the workspace (`isPathWithinWorkspace`, `chunk-RCB6BI5H.js:351390`). Measured on the first request at the stub, with the §2.3.2 layout, GEMINI.md canaries in the workspace root, a workspace subdirectory, the Gemini home, every ancestor of the cwd and the test root, and a `.git` in the test root: (1) revision 4's settings, `tools.core=[]`, with `--include-directories=<ws>`: no tools, but the workspace and every ancestor canary, a workspace `@` import of the home's `oauth_creds.json`, and the directory tree; (2) the same without `--include-directories`: every ancestor canary and the tree; (3) with `memoryBoundaryMarkers=[]` and `includeDirectoryTree=false`, without `--include-directories`: no canary and no tree; (4) the same with `--include-directories=<ws>`, at `none` and `read_only` with the `.git` and at `read_only` without it: only the workspace-root canary and an import of a file inside the workspace, no ancestor canary, no credential and no tree; (5) as (4) at `read_only` with the workspace `GEMINI.md` a symlink to the home's `oauth_creds.json`, or to `/proc/self/environ`: the credential, or the env with `GEMINI_API_KEY`, is in the request. | Bundle `chunk-RCB6BI5H.js:365013-365025` (`getEnvironmentMemoryPaths`), `365034-365063` (`findUpwardGeminiFiles`), `365065-365134` (JIT), `365168-365175` (`discoverMemoryPaths`), `364879-364882` (`findProjectRoot2` returns `null` for `[]`), `364592-364595, 364668-364672, 364783-364805` (imports: `findProjectRoot`, `validateImportPath` on real paths), `364914-364922` (`readGeminiMdFiles`), `337621` (tree); `chunk-VFM6AETN.js:35147-35198` (schema); `chunk-ASDLQWJU.js:56261` (the reload command, the only reader of `loadMemoryFromIncludeDirectories`); Gemini stub captures, cases (1) to (5). |
| **The real context of each Gemini level, startup and JIT (revision 6, rerun in new directories).** Layout: a test root holding `.git`; under it the runtime root, `provider-homes/agent1/spawns/<incarnation>/<spawn>` (the spawn home, its 0500 `cwd/` with the stop file, and an `oauth_creds.json` canary), and `workspaces/agent1` (the workspace); a GEMINI.md canary in the test root, the runtime root, the agent's provider-home directory, `spawns/`, the spawn home, `workspaces/`, the workspace root, `sub/` and `sub/deeper/`; the workspace GEMINI.md also `@`-imports the credential canary by absolute path, relative path and symlink. With the §2.3.2 settings of each level (`memoryBoundaryMarkers: []`, `includeDirectoryTree: false`): `none` (no `--include-directories`): no tools, no canary, no tree. `read_only`, `workspace_write`, `workspace_exec`: the level's `tools.core`, only the workspace-root canary and an import of a file inside the workspace, no tree, no credential. With the stub answering the first request with `read_file` of `sub/deeper/file.txt` at `read_only`, the follow-up's function response adds the `sub/deeper` and `sub` canaries under "Newly Discovered Project Context", and no canary above the workspace. Positive control, the same runs without `memoryBoundaryMarkers`: at `none` the spawn home's and every ancestor's canary load (the climb starts at the cwd); at `read_only` every ancestor's canary and the credential (through the import, whose boundary is then the `.git` root) load, at startup and in the JIT response. ---

## 2. Decision

### 2.1 Invariants

1. **Koda policy is the only authority.** A provider built-in tool runs only at a level the agent's signed, published policy pre-authorises, lowered by every ceiling that applies, and only on a provider whose capability the published policy covers. Nothing raises it.
2. **Per-call approval exists only on the governed path.** Built-in tools have no approval state, because the CLIs run headless. Anything that needs a human goes through `<agent_cmd>`, `evaluate_execution_policy` and the approval pipeline (§2.9).
3. **Koda confines model-directed code.** Every process that runs code the model controls, or code it can plant, runs inside Koda's confinement or does not run, except where a residual of §2.5.4 names the gap (`off`, R-8; the unsupervised start, R-9; operator commands, and model-written shell jobs if OQ-11 (a) is declined, before AG-15, or for good if OQ-9 (b) is declined, R-11; the kernel's own git, tar and copy where the helper cannot confine them, R-11a; a work dir the model chooses under HOME if OQ-12 (a) is declined, or the install directory if (b) is, R-14). A provider's own sandbox is an inner layer, used only where it has been proven to start inside the same confinement.
4. **Never wider than the inner layer allows.** Koda never asks a provider sandbox for more than the effective level, except where Koda's own L2 enforces the level instead. In `RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT=off` the provider's own restriction is the only layer, and no level is weaker than today's behaviour.
5. **The model cannot reach control-plane secrets.** No process the model can reach holds or can read the master key, the bootstrap code, the worker's secrets, another agent's data or an unauthenticated control endpoint. Residuals, including everything `off` gives up (R-8), are listed in §2.5.4.
6. **Fail closed.** An unverified CLI version, a missing flag, an unproven restriction, a settings warning or a missing kernel feature makes the capability unavailable, and the reason is shown. It never silently widens.

### 2.2 Governance model

#### 2.2.1 Field

- `execution_policy.provider_builtin_tools.level` is an ordered enum: `none < read_only < workspace_write < workspace_exec`. It is a typed block compiled at spawn, not a matched rule. There are no per-provider overrides and no free-form tool lists; those break across CLI versions.
- **Default `read_only`.** A missing block or key means `read_only`. It matches the governed path's baseline, where reads by enabled tools are allowed and writes need approval (`services/execution_policy.py:532-575`), and reads stay cheap.
- **One delivery channel.** The published, signed `AGENT_SPEC_JSON` (AG-10a, AG-11) carries the execution policy **and the model policy**. `AGENT_EXECUTION_POLICY_JSON` and its readers are deleted. The worker reads neither drafts nor live control-plane rows for governance. AG-10a moves the execution policy, and deletes the `skills/_runtime.py` draft fallback (a missing `AGENT_SPEC_JSON` gives no custom skills, as the one accessor gives secure defaults). AG-17b moves the computation of the worker's runtime settings view (`_effective_agent_runtime_settings`, `agent_settings.py:232-260`) into `app`, unchanged, behind `GET /internal/agents/runtime-settings` on the AG-17 token; the worker keeps its 5 s cache of the answer. AG-21b then moves the model policy and the chat-settable `providers` keys (`agent_settings.py:232-236`) to the published snapshot, in the same merge that makes chat commands publish (§2.2.3): from then on `app` computes that view from the agent's published snapshot and the install's global settings. AG-22a moves the four squad paths that read another agent's draft through `get_agent_spec` to that agent's **published** snapshot: the planner (`coordinator_engine.py:423`), the `squad_coordinator_elect` tool (`tool_dispatcher.py:4937`), the coordinator service's eligibility check (`squads/coordinator.py:166-173`, which reads `tool_policy`) and membership's capability hints (`squads/membership.py:214`). They read it through `GET /internal/agents/{agent}/published-spec` on the AG-17 token. `app` reads the target's published version (and, from AG-11, verifies its MAC) and returns a closed field list: `model_policy`, `tool_policy.allowed_tool_ids`, and the spec fields `build_capability_summary` reads. The target must be the token's own agent or an agent in its squad. Neither route reads a draft, and the worker reads no `cp_agent_config_versions` row. AG-22a also moves the `runtime_endpoint_json` write out of `build_draft_snapshot` (`manager.py:12509-12513`) into `publish_agent`, so building a spec writes nothing and the worker role can lose its write on `cp_agent_definitions` (M1-E2-T5).
- **One resolver**, `koda/services/provider_builtin_tools.py`: `normalize`, `validate`, and `resolve(published, provider, ctx) -> BuiltinToolProfile{requested, effective, clamps[], capability, process_class, refusal}`. The runtime, publish validation, the prompt preview and the simulator import it. The control-plane normalizer delegates this block to it.
- The name avoids "native tools", which means Koda tools sent as function-calling schemas (`docs/architecture/tool-registry-native-tools.md`).

#### 2.2.2 Schema

Authored by the operator, in the draft and the published spec:

```json
{
  "$schema": "https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/schema",
  "$id": "koda:execution_policy.provider_builtin_tools",
  "type": "object",
  "additionalProperties": false,
  "properties": {
    "level": { "enum": ["none", "read_only", "workspace_write", "workspace_exec"], "default": "read_only" }
  }
}
```

- Validation errors use the existing execution-policy error shape: `{"path": "execution_policy.provider_builtin_tools.level", "code": "invalid_enum", "message": ...}`, and `code: "unknown_key"` for any other key.
- The block has no version key. It is versioned with the snapshot; an incompatible change would add a new key, never a new meaning for `level`.

**Actor.** One shape everywhere (the gate's parameter, the governance block, the audit event):

```json
{
  "$id": "koda:actor",
  "type": "object",
  "additionalProperties": false,
  "required": ["kind", "id"],
  "properties": {
    "kind": { "enum": ["operator", "system", "system:migration", "system:import", "telegram_user"] },
    "id": { "type": "string", "description": "operator account id, Telegram user id, or the system component (supervisor, bootstrap, runtime_snapshot, AG-21 migration, legacy import)" }
  }
}
```

Derived at publish by `publish_agent` (never authored; a draft that contains it is rejected), inside the signed snapshot:

```json
"governance": {
  "provider_builtin_tools": {
    "level": "workspace_write",
    "capability_ceiling": ["read", "write"],
    "providers": ["claude", "gemini"],
    "published_by": { "kind": "operator", "id": "7" },
    "confirmed": true,
    "published_at": "2026-11-20T10:00:00Z",
    "previous_version": 41
  }
}
```

`providers` is every provider the published model policy lets the agent run: the default provider, the fallbacks and the functional defaults. `capability_ceiling` is the union of `capability(provider, level)` over them (§2.2.3).

#### 2.2.3 Capability, escalation and the publish gate

**Capability.** The contract table records, per pinned CLI version, provider and level, a conservative capability set over `read` (file reads), `write` (workspace writes), `exec` (model-chosen programs) and `egress` (model-chosen network connections), taken as the widest over the confinement modes:

| Provider | `none` | `read_only` | `workspace_write` | `workspace_exec` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Claude | `[]` | `[read]` | `[read, write]` | `[read, write, exec, egress]` |
| Codex 0.169.0 | `[]` | `[]` (clamped to `none`, §2.3.3) | `[read, write, exec]` (only under `off`, R-8; clamped to `none` under L2) | `[read, write, exec, egress]` |
| Gemini | `[]` (no workspace directory, no tree, no climb, §2.3.2) | `[read]` (plus the startup and JIT GEMINI.md of R-7, and the symlinked GEMINI.md of R-1) | `[read, write]` (with the same two GEMINI.md residuals) | `[read, write, exec, egress]` |

**Escalation** is a publish whose level is higher than the baseline's, or whose `capability_ceiling` is not a subset of the baseline's. Adding Codex to a Claude `workspace_write` agent adds `exec` and is an escalation; raising the level is one; adding Codex to a Claude `read_only` agent is not, because Codex has no tools at `read_only`.

**Runtime check (S10).** A turn on provider P at effective level L runs only if `capability(P, L)` is a subset of the published `capability_ceiling`. Otherwise P is refused for the turn with `capability_not_confirmed`, the fallback chain skips it, and an alert is raised. Between AG-21 and AG-21b the worker still reads the draft model policy, as it does today (C9). In that window S10 is what keeps a provider added by a chat command from running at a capability nobody confirmed. From AG-21b on, the model policy comes only from the published snapshot, so the ceiling covers every provider it names, and capability is monotone in the level. S10 can then fail only after a CLI bump changes the capability table, or through a bug, and it stays as that assertion. Internal calls run at `none`, whose capability is empty, so they pass on any provider. A version without a governance block has the ceiling `capability(its providers, read_only)` (step 2).

**Publish gate.** `publish_agent(agent_id, *, actor, confirm_escalation=False, change=None)` takes a required `actor` (§2.2.2). It is a compare-and-swap on the version number, because the control-plane store has no multi-statement transaction: every `fetch_one`, `fetch_all` and `execute` takes its own pooled connection and autocommits (`koda/state/control_plane_store.py:60-97`; `koda/state/primary.py:330-388`), and `with_connection` replays statements one by one (`control_plane_store.py:106-120`).

- The gate reads the last published version `P` (its number and snapshot). With no lock held, it builds the source, validates it and computes the baseline from `P` (steps 1 to 3).
- Step 4 writes in one short transaction on one connection: version `P+1`, `desired_version = P+1`, the apply operation and the `agent.provider_builtin_tools_changed` event. It uses AG-07's transaction helper (§2.6).
- A concurrent publish of the same agent that wrote `P+1` first makes the insert fail on `UNIQUE (agent_id, version)` (`koda/knowledge/v2/postgres_backend.py:1362-1371`), and nothing of this attempt is written. The gate then re-reads the last version and runs steps 1 to 4 once more: a chat patch is re-applied to the new last published snapshot, and the check runs against the new baseline.
- An operator escalation is retried only when the capabilities it adds are the ones the operator confirmed. Otherwise, or when the second attempt also loses, the publish fails with `publish_conflict` and writes nothing. The route answers 409, and the modal or the chat reply asks to try again.

A chat patch is therefore always applied to the latest published snapshot and checked against the latest baseline; it can never republish an older snapshot over an operator's newer one (§5.1.10). Nothing is locked while the draft is built, so the build's own write to `cp_agent_definitions` (`manager.py:12509-12513`, until AG-22a moves it) cannot wait on the gate.

1. **Source.** Only an `operator` publish takes the draft as it is. No other actor can publish the operator's pending policy:
   - `telegram_user` applies only its own `change` to the **last published snapshot**. The change is a field-level patch from a closed list:
     - `voice_policy.mode`;
     - `model_policy.default_provider`, `model_policy.default_models.<provider>` and `model_policy.functional_defaults.<function>`;
     - `providers.elevenlabs_default_voice`, `providers.elevenlabs_default_language`, `providers.kokoro_default_voice` and `providers.kokoro_default_language`. These voice keys carry no capability.

     The same patch is written to the draft, so the dashboard shows it.
   - `system` publishes only an agent that has **no version at all**, through the supervisor's reconcile (bootstrap, which publishes from an unsupervised worker today, never publishes from AG-21 on; see the caller table). It takes the draft with `execution_policy` and `tool_policy` replaced by the defaults a new agent gets: no rules, level `read_only`, the secure-default tool subset. The dashboard shows the operator's policy as pending until an operator publishes it. A `system` publish of an agent that has any row in `cp_agent_config_versions` is refused with `system_publish_of_published_agent`. It is never retried with secure defaults, so resetting `desired_version` to 0 cannot republish the draft.
   - `system:migration` publishes the last published snapshot with only the governance block or the signature added (AG-21, AG-11). `system:import` publishes the imported legacy configuration at level `read_only`, which only lowers what those agents ran with.
   - **Validation follows the source.** Each actor validates only what it publishes. `operator` runs `publish_checks` on the draft, as today (`manager.py:12569-12572`). `system` runs it on the draft with the secure-default policies, and `system:import` on the imported configuration. `telegram_user` is validated by the settings route's value checks on its patch, and nothing else. `system:migration` republishes a snapshot that is already published and runs no validation beyond the gate. So draft-only errors (an enabled general provider whose CLI is missing, a granted secret that no longer exists, a runtime prompt-budget overflow, a missing `AGENT_TOKEN`, C9) block only operator publishes and the first `system` publish, never a chat change, the AG-21 record or the AG-11 re-sign (§5.1.10).
2. **Baseline.** The last published version's governance block. A version without a governance block (published before AG-21) reads as `{level: read_only, capability_ceiling: capability(its providers, read_only)}`, for every actor, for S10 and for the resolver. That is what a missing block already means (§2.2.1), so no "unrestricted" baseline exists and no publish waits for a migration. An agent never published has the baseline `{level: read_only, capability_ceiling: [read]}`.
3. **Check.** On escalation, refuse unless `actor.kind == operator` and `confirm_escalation`. The error names the new capabilities. For `telegram_user` the reply is "A troca precisa de confirmação no painel: {capacidades}.", and the patch stays in the draft.
4. **Write** the version `P+1`, the governance block (signed with the snapshot, AG-11), `desired_version`, the apply operation and the `agent.provider_builtin_tools_changed` event (§2.6.1), in one transaction on one connection. A unique-constraint conflict is retried once as described above. The publish fails if the event cannot be written.
5. **The AG-21 migration is a record, not a precondition.** At control-plane start (next to `ensure_master_key`, `supervisor.py:223`), for each agent whose last published version has no governance block, it publishes that snapshot with the explicit block at `read_only` and writes one event with `reason: migration`. Nothing waits for it: an agent it has not reached already runs at `read_only` (step 2), and any publish of that agent works. A failure leaves the health reason `governance_migration_failed:<agent>` and is retried at the next control-plane start; an operator publish of that agent writes the block and the event itself.

**Where publishing runs.** `publish_agent` and signing run only in `app` (the control plane and its supervisor).

- From AG-11 on, the snapshot signer is built only by the control-plane process. `publish_agent` without it raises `publish_requires_control_plane`, and the supervisor refuses a version row that carries no valid MAC (§2.5.3). A worker-side call can therefore never produce a version that runs (A-P-07). Bootstrap stops calling it in AG-21, on which AG-11 depends, so an unsupervised start never meets that error.
- AG-11 depends on AG-17b, which moves every supervised worker's publish into `app`, so no release ever signs in a supervised worker.
- The runtime-snapshot fallback publish is deleted from `_resolve_runtime_snapshot` (`manager.py:12633-12636`). A missing version raises `no published snapshot` (`:12637-12638`), and the supervisor skips that agent, so nothing is published.

**Chat settings through the control plane (AG-17b moves them, AG-21b sets what they publish).** Telegram updates reach the worker, so the worker forwards chat settings changes to a broker route on the control-plane API:

- AG-17b moves every chat setter's draft write and publish, and the runtime-settings read, behind the two routes without changing what they do: `app` writes the draft as the setter does today, publishes where the setter publishes today (the voice toggle and the voice and audio defaults, through the gate once AG-21 is in), and computes the runtime settings as `_effective_agent_runtime_settings` does today. It needs no gate. AG-21b, gated by OQ-2, then changes what `app` does, as the rest of this list describes.

- `POST /internal/agents/settings-change` is authenticated by the AG-17 per-worker token. The agent always comes from the token, never from the body.
- The body is `{"telegram_user_id": int, "patch": [{"path": <one of the closed list>, "value": …}]}`. The control plane validates each value: a provider it knows, a model in that provider's catalog for the function, a voice it knows. It checks that the asserted Telegram user is on the agent's allowed-user list as the control plane resolves it, the same check as the approval relay (§2.9.2).
- In one call the control plane writes the patch to the draft and calls `publish_agent(agent, actor={"kind": "telegram_user", "id": <user>}, change=patch)`. That publish applies the patch to the last published snapshot, runs the gate and signs.
- The route answers `published` (with the version), `needs_confirmation` (with the capabilities added; the patch stays in the draft) or `refused` (with the reason). The worker turns the answer into the chat reply.
- Every chat setter uses the route: the voice toggle, the voice and audio defaults, `/provider`, `/model`, the functional defaults, the settings-menu buttons (`callbacks.py:262, 288, 1403`), and the natural-language parser that every plain message passes first (C9).
- Session state follows the answer. `callback_model` changes the session's provider model and turns `auto_model` off (`callbacks.py:266-267`); the worker does that, and any other per-session change a setter makes, only on `published`. On `needs_confirmation` or `refused` the session keeps its provider and model.
- The worker then reads the model policy and the `providers` voice keys only from its published snapshot.
- Like the Telegram approval relay, this route is a resolution the **worker asserts** (§2.9.2, R-4). Any code holding the worker's broker token can change that agent's settings within the closed list. It cannot raise a capability, because the gate refuses `telegram_user` escalations, and it cannot reach another agent.

**A published chat change restarts the worker.** Every published change creates a new version, and the kernel restarts a worker whose version changed (C17).

- A turn in progress gets up to the kernel's 15 s grace to finish and is then stopped: the kernel kills the worker, and each of its spawns ends with it, the CLI and every process the model started through it. That includes the CLIs and governed shell commands the kernel itself started for that worker (C17): they keep running through the grace, and the kernel ends them once the worker is gone and before it spawns the new worker (§2.3.5, "Spawn lifetime" and "Spawns the kernel starts"; R-10 names the cases where such a process can outlive it). So nothing a spawn of the old version started runs on into the new one, and a lowered level reaches the model's processes too. Running and queued tasks are re-queued by the new worker. Each restart counts as one recovery, so a task interrupted by `QUEUE_MAX_RECOVERY_ATTEMPTS` restarts (3, `config.py:1374`) goes to the DLQ, as any recovered task does today (C17). The new worker resolves each turn's level and ceiling at turn start from the new published version, never from values recorded at enqueue. A provider recorded at enqueue runs only if that version covers it (S10); otherwise the published chain applies.
- Pending approvals: until M3-E3-T2, a restart restores them from their JSON files, as today (D-163). From M3-E3-T2 on, they expire (§2.9.2; D12-06).
- The voice toggle and the audio defaults already restart the worker today. From AG-21b, `/provider`, `/model`, the functional defaults, the settings-menu buttons and the natural-language path do too. The chat reply says so: "Alteração publicada. O agente reinicia e aplica a mudança a partir da próxima mensagem." From M3-E3-T2 on it adds " Aprovações pendentes expiram." A-P-06 tests the reply and the re-queue.
- Dashboard draft edits stop taking effect without a publish. Today a draft edit of the model policy or the `providers` section reaches the running worker the next time a chat settings command, menu or setter forces a refresh from the draft (C17). From AG-21b on, turn-time reads keep the published `AGENT_SPEC_JSON`, and a forced refresh calls `GET /internal/agents/runtime-settings` (§2.2.1), which `app` computes from the published snapshot. Such an edit therefore takes effect when the operator publishes. The notice says so (§2.8.4).

Every publish path calls the one gate function (`manager.py:12568`):

| Caller | Runs in | Actor | Source | On escalation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Publish route (`koda/control_plane/api.py:3085-3095`) | `app` | `operator`; `confirm_escalation` comes from the body and is sent only by the escalation modal | draft | publishes when confirmed |
| Onboarding (`manager.py:5909`) | `app` | `operator`; the onboarding step shows the modal when the chosen provider's `read_only` capability exceeds `[read]` (none does on the pinned CLIs) | draft | publishes when confirmed |
| Supervisor reconcile, first publish (`supervisor.py:458-461`) | `app` | `system` | draft with secure-default execution and tool policies; refused when the agent has any version | refused; the agent shows "Aguardando publicação" |
| Runtime-snapshot fallback (`manager.py:12633-12636`; reached from `supervisor.py:473` and `bootstrap.py:58`) | `app`, and unsupervised workers | — | **deleted**: a missing version raises, and nothing is published | — |
| Bootstrap (`koda/control_plane/bootstrap.py:53-56`), only in a worker started without the supervisor (C17) | worker | none from AG-21: it never publishes, and an unsupervised start of a never-published agent fails with `agent_not_published`. Before AG-21 it publishes as today. AG-21 lands before AG-11, which makes a worker-side `publish_agent` raise, and before AG-21b. A published agent still starts unsupervised, in the development mode of R-9 (§2.5.4) | — | — |
| Legacy import (`manager.py:13101`) | `app` | `system:import` | imported configuration at `read_only` | any other raise is refused |
| Voice toggle (`commands.py:2447` → `agent_settings.py:495-508`) | worker → settings route (AG-17b); in-process before AG-17b | `telegram_user` | last published + `voice_policy.mode` | n/a (no capability change) |
| Voice default (`commands.py:2398`; `chat_settings.py:273` → `agent_settings.py:619-695`) and ElevenLabs model (`commands.py:2059` → `agent_settings.py:581-617`) | as above | `telegram_user` | last published + `voice_policy.mode`, the `providers` voice keys and `model_policy.functional_defaults.audio` | n/a (audio providers have no built-in tools, so their capability is `[]`) |
| `/provider` (`commands.py:447`; `chat_settings.py:196` → `agent_settings.py:517-553`), `/model` (`commands.py:510`; `chat_settings.py:224` → `:555-578`), functional defaults (`commands.py:587`; `chat_settings.py:256` → `:581-617`) | worker → settings route from AG-17b, where `app` writes the draft as the setter does today; they publish from AG-21b. Before AG-21b they only write the draft, and S10 guards the turn | `telegram_user` | last published + the model-policy patch | refused, patch kept in the draft |
| Natural-language settings (`messages.py:29` → `chat_settings.py:278`), reached by every plain chat message | worker → settings route (AG-17b), through the setters above | `telegram_user` | as the setter it calls | as the setter it calls |
| Settings-menu buttons: `callback_model` (`callbacks.py:262`, then `:266-267`), `callback_provider` (`:288`), `callback_feature_model_model` (`:1403`) | worker → settings route (AG-17b), through the setters above | `telegram_user` | as the setter it calls | as the setter it calls; the session keeps its provider and model |
| AG-21 migration (a record, step 5) | `app` | `system:migration` | last published + governance block at `read_only`; no draft check | n/a (equal to the block-less baseline) |
| AG-11 re-signing | `app` | `system:migration` | last published, unchanged, signed; no draft check | n/a |

`clone_agent` (`manager.py:9937`) copies the execution policy into a new draft. Its first operator publish compares with the never-published baseline, so a cloned `workspace_exec` policy needs confirmation.

#### 2.2.4 Koda policy states and ceilings

The effective level is the minimum of the published level and every applicable ceiling. The matrix in §2.3 names these states.

| State | Signal | Ceiling | Replaces |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | Published level (default `read_only`) | — | — |
| S2 | Scheduled dry run | `read_only` | `queue_manager.py:3122`; `claude_runner.py:92-96`; `codex_runner.py:286`; `gemini_runner.py:86-88` |
| S3 | Blocking artifact gap | `read_only` | `queue_manager.py:3465-3467` |
| S4 | Child run, any toolset | `read_only` | `queue_manager.py:3593-3600` |
| S5 | Effective `approval_mode` ∈ {`read_only`, `escalation_required`} | `read_only` | new (never reached the CLI) |
| S6 | `agent_mode == supervised` | `read_only` | `queue_manager.py:3123` |
| S7 | Internal call site: memory extractor, squad planner | `none` on every provider, with a fresh, empty per-task work dir and `TMPDIR` under the task tmp (never `/tmp`, HOME or `DEFAULT_WORK_DIR`). The extractor keeps its install-wide chain; the planner uses the coordinator's published model policy. | `memory/extractor.py:183-193`; `coordinator_engine.py:375-385, 419-437` |
| S8 | Level not faithful on this CLI, host and confinement profile (the contract; the Codex probe `IP` run inside the spawn's own profile; Codex's unconfined reads; a Codex model whose `none` is unproven, §2.3.3; a settings warning or a failed Gemini probe) | next lower faithful level; below `none`, the provider is refused for the turn | — |
| S9 | Class M level while the confinement mode's required layers are missing (from AG-14 on). A spawn the kernel starts (C17) counts as missing them until AG-15 puts it through the helper | the highest class S level; governed class M processes (shell, `shell_bg`, git, `cli_runner`, and, with OQ-11 (a), a shell job whose command or work dir the model wrote, §2.9.1) are refused. Operator commands and operator-authored scheduled shell jobs (§2.9.1) are not governed calls, so S9 does not refuse them: before AG-15 they run as today (R-11), and from AG-15 under the class M profile, refused only where that profile cannot be enforced (OQ-9) | — |
| S10 | `capability(P, L)` not covered by the published ceiling (internal calls included) | provider refused for the turn; an assertion (§2.2.3) | — |

- The task-kind default `supervised` approval mode (`knowledge/task_policy_defaults.py`) is deliberately not a ceiling: general tasks default to it, so it would make every opt-in inert, and it is derived from the query text. Ceilings may come from such signals only because they only lower.
- Below the floor (`none` on every provider), the provider is refused for the turn; the level is never raised.
- **Supervised mode means the same on every provider:** built-ins capped at `read_only`; every governed write needs a human grant (§2.9.1); Koda's loop pauses after each governed round with the existing Continue/Stop buttons (`queue_manager.py:6513-6522`). The Claude-only `--max-turns 1` coupling is removed.
- **Governed-loop budget.** `MAX_AGENT_TOOL_ITERATIONS` stays the install-wide ceiling (operator setting `manager.py:650`; default 8, `config.py:1326`), and `max_budget` also stops the loop. The default changes only on M0-E3-T2 eval evidence (AG-27).
- **Evaluator.** AG-06 builds one engine with fnmatch for every rule; precedence is the most restrictive decision first (deny > require_approval > allow_with_preview > allow), then priority, then name; a rule's name is its audit `rule_id`; simulate and preview call it. This is ADR-039, which amends ADR-008 step 1 and moves its replay safeguard to delivery: AG-09 replays every stored policy under the legacy matcher and the engine before AG-10b activates rules. It changes nothing in production while rules are undelivered (C6).

### 2.3 Provider mode matrix

Each runner compiles the profile from the one contract table in `provider_cli_contract.py`, keyed by pinned version. The probes check every flag against `--help` (or the parser probe for hidden flags). Codex config keys are probed for existence and type (A-K-01), Codex features against a reviewed list (A-K-02), and Codex's model-visible tools and its refusal of forced calls offline for every configured model (A-K-04). Each exact Gemini settings file Koda writes is probed offline for loading without a warning, each of its keys outside `telemetry` for existence and type, and the first request for the startup context it carries (A-K-05). A level whose restriction depends on an unproven key or setting is unavailable (S8) until its live check passes.

#### 2.3.1 Claude Code 2.1.325

**Every level:**

```
claude -p --output-format json|stream-json [--verbose] --model M --max-turns N
  [--max-budget-usd B] [--effort E] [--resume ID]
  --restricted --safe-mode --strict-mcp-config --disable-slash-commands
  --permission-prompts none --permission-mode dontAsk
  --tools <T> --allowedTools <T>
  --append-system-prompt-file <spawn home>/system.md      (0600, created with O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW in the new
                                                           spawn home, removed with it; never the task tmp)
  [--add-dir <task_tmp>/images]
cwd: the workspace; for S7 internal calls, a fresh empty per-task dir
env (allowlisted builder, AG-03): CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1; CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME=koda (§2.3.5);
     HOME, CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR and XDG_* inside the spawn's Claude home (§2.3.5); TMPDIR = the task tmp
```

`--strict-mcp-config` is passed with no `--mcp-config`, so no MCP server loads. `--add-dir` covers only the task's image directory. The system prompt file lives in the spawn home for the reason the homes are not under the task tmp (§2.3.5): one task spawns Claude several times (each governed round is a resume, plus verification turns and fallbacks), and governed shell and git, `shell_bg` included, are granted the task tmp read-write under L2, so a background shell of an earlier round could rewrite or pre-plant the next spawn's prompt there. The spawn home is new, random and granted only to its own CLI process, and Koda creates the file before that process starts, with `O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, so a link at the path fails the create instead of being written through (`[V]`, §1.2). Claude reads the file at start (`[V]`: its canary is in the request's `system` field).

| Level | `<T>` | Selected by | Class |
|---|---|---|---|
| `none` | `""` | S1 = `none`; S7 | S |
| `read_only` | `Read,Grep,Glob` | S1 = `read_only` (default); S2 to S6 | S once AG-23 proves path confinement, else M |
| `workspace_write` | `Read,Grep,Glob,Edit,Write` | S1 = `workspace_write` with no lowering state; S9 for `workspace_exec` without L2 | S once proven, else M |
| `workspace_exec` | `Read,Grep,Glob,Edit,Write,Bash` | S1 = `workspace_exec` with no lowering state | M (needs AG-14) |

**One row per Claude permission mode:**

| `--permission-mode` | Koda state that selects it | Argv | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `dontAsk` | Every state, at every level | the block above | Denies anything not pre-approved; the level is expressed only by `--tools`/`--allowedTools`. |
| `default` (alias `manual`) | None | not emitted; `validate_args` refuses it once AG-23 narrows the choices to `dontAsk` | Prompts, and headless prompts are denied by `--permission-prompts none`, so it adds nothing over `dontAsk` and is less explicit. |
| `acceptEdits` | None | not emitted | Auto-accepts edits as a mode rather than through the explicit list; `workspace_write` already lists Edit and Write. |
| `auto` | None | not emitted | A model classifier approves prompts: an authority other than Koda policy (invariant 1). |
| `plan` | None. Today: S2, S3, S4 and the memory extractor (C1) | not emitted | Replaced by explicit `read_only`/`none` tool lists; one mode keeps one semantics across versions. |
| `bypassPermissions` | None. Today: every other turn (C1) | not emitted; `--restricted` also refuses it | Invariant 1. |

Never emitted at all: `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, `--allow-dangerously-skip-permissions`, `--mcp-config`, `--setting-sources`, `--settings`, `--plugin-dir`, `--plugin-url`, `--agents`, `--bare` (it reads only `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, so subscription logins break) and `--append-system-prompt` (the prompt stays off argv). Floor: `none`.

#### 2.3.2 Gemini CLI 0.68.0

**Every level** (attached values only; `validate_args` refuses separate values, because yargs reads a separate value that starts with `-` as an option, VALIDATION_REPORT WAVE-2B):

```
gemini --model=M --output-format=json|text --extensions=none --approval-mode=<A>
       [--include-directories=<workspace>]        (read_only and above only; omitted at none, which covers S7;
                                                    a workspace path containing "," is refused)
cwd   = <spawn home>/cwd: mode 0500, created with the spawn's own home (§2.3.5); it holds only the
        stop file .gemini/.env (empty, 0400, in a 0500 .gemini/), written by Koda
stdin = <system_instructions>…</system_instructions><user_request>{escaped query}</user_request>
env   : HOME = GEMINI_CLI_HOME = the spawn's Gemini home; GEMINI_CLI_TRUST_WORKSPACE=true; TMPDIR = the task tmp;
        pinned: GEMINI_SYSTEM_MD=false, GEMINI_TELEMETRY_ENABLED=false, GEMINI_TELEMETRY_LOG_PROMPTS=false,
                GEMINI_TELEMETRY_OTLP_ENDPOINT="", OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT="",
                GOOGLE_GEMINI_BASE_URL=<the configured gateway or "">, GOOGLE_VERTEX_BASE_URL="",
                GEMINI_API_KEY, GOOGLE_API_KEY, GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT, GOOGLE_CLOUD_LOCATION = configured value or "",
                GEMINI_CLI_SYSTEM_SETTINGS_PATH=/dev/null/koda-no-system-settings.json,
                GEMINI_CLI_SYSTEM_DEFAULTS_PATH=/dev/null/koda-no-system-defaults.json
settings: <spawn home>/.gemini/settings.json, written by Koda (0600) for this spawn only; no system settings file
```

- **Why the workspace is not the cwd.** Gemini reads workspace settings only from the cwd, and starts its `.env` search there (§1.2). With a Koda-owned cwd, a planted or repository-supplied `.gemini/settings.json`, `.gemini/.env` or `.env` in the workspace is never read, whatever its content (`context.includeDirectories`, hooks, MCP servers, discovery commands, telemetry, a base URL or a system prompt).
- **Why the cwd holds a stop file.** Gemini's search does not stop at the cwd. It walks every ancestor up to `/`, loads the first `.gemini/.env` it finds, and sets every key in it that is not already set (§1.2). The cwd's ancestors are:
  - the spawn home, `provider-homes/<agent>/spawns` and `provider-homes/<agent>`;
  - Koda's runtime root and everything above it. On native installs after AG-12 the runtime root is `~/.koda-runtime/<bid>`, so the ancestors include the operator's own HOME. `~/.gemini/.env`, where Gemini users keep personal keys, would then flow into every agent's run. In compose they include `/var/lib/koda/runtime` (`docker-compose.yml:57`), on the `koda-runtime` volume that `runtime-kernel` mounts read-write (`:250`).
  
  Under L2 no confined process can write any of these directories. Under `off`, and before AG-14, an unconfined class M process could plant a file there that changes every agent's Gemini at every level, and that no spawn home removes. The pins do not prevent this, because they are a denylist. Any unpinned variable loads: `CODE_ASSIST_ENDPOINT` (the OAuth endpoint) and `GOOGLE_GENAI_USE_VERTEXAI` did in the probe (§1.2), and by the same code path so would `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` or `HTTPS_PROXY`. So every spawn home gets an empty `cwd/.gemini/.env`. The search finds it in the first directory it examines and reads nothing else, in every confinement mode (verified: no ancestor or home file loaded, no warning). The exposure is new with this design: today's runner does not trust the workspace, so Gemini reads only whitelisted auth keys, and only from a plain `.env` (`chunk-VFM6AETN.js:37777, 38173-38178`; `findEnvFile` checks `.gemini/.env` only when trusted).
