- The supervisor verifies before building a worker env: the MAC, that `agent_id` and `version` in the MAC match the row and the agent being spawned (so another agent's signed snapshot is refused), and that the version is not lower than the highest version it has verified for that agent since it started (rollback while `app` runs is refused). On a failure it does not spawn and emits `security.snapshot_rejected`.
- **Only `app` signs.** The signer is built only by the control-plane process, and `publish_agent` without it raises `publish_requires_control_plane`. AG-11 depends on AG-17b, so by the time snapshots are signed, no chat path publishes from a supervised worker (§2.2.3).
- Versions published before AG-11 carry no MAC. At control-plane start, AG-11 republishes each agent's unsigned latest version unchanged and signed (actor `system:migration`, through AG-21's actor-aware primitive, which never reads the draft), trusting the row as it is at upgrade, as today. It runs no draft check (§2.2.3 step 1), so an invalid draft cannot stop it. If it still fails for an agent (a database error, or no signing key), the supervisor does not spawn that agent's unsigned version: health shows `snapshot_unsigned:<agent>`, the re-sign is retried on every supervisor reconcile pass (every `CONTROL_PLANE_POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS`, `supervisor.py:433`), and an operator publish of the agent also produces a signed version.
- **What the MAC protects, and from when.** It stops a holder of the owner-role DSN who cannot compute the signing key. Until AG-17 (workers stop loading the master key to decrypt) and AG-19 (`koda-state` leaves `runtime-kernel`) both ship, a worker can read the key material and forge a MAC (R-5); on native installs it always can (§2.5.2).
- A DSN holder can still, across an `app` restart, delete newer version rows or point `desired_version` at an older signed version (`manager.py:12600-12607`; `supervisor.py:459`). That is residual R-5. M1-E2-T5 closes it, with its scope extended by this ADR: the worker role gets no write on `cp_agent_config_versions` or `cp_agent_definitions`, which is possible once AG-17b has moved every worker publish and draft write to `app` and AG-22a has removed the endpoint write from `build_draft_snapshot` (C9). A persisted high-water mark was rejected: restoring an older database backup would then refuse every agent.
- Live worker reads of `cp_mcp_tool_policies` (`services/execution_policy.py:127-165`) move into the snapshot (O-5).

#### 2.5.4 Documented residuals

| # | Residual | Closed by |
|---|---|---|
| R-1 | The CLI's own credential is readable by the model: through any shell (Codex `workspace_write` under `off` and `workspace_exec`; Claude and Gemini at `workspace_exec`), and at `read_only` and `workspace_write` through Claude's Read or Gemini's `read_file` (`/proc/self/environ`, the spawn-home credential files) until AG-23 and AG-25 prove the file tools' path confinement. For Gemini 0.68.0 there is a second path at `read_only` and above that those checks cannot close: a workspace GEMINI.md that is a symlink to `/proc/self/environ` or to the spawn home's credential file is read into the request at startup, and by JIT discovery from subdirectories (`[V]`, §1.2). Planting it needs a write to the workspace: an earlier turn at a write level, governed git or file tools, or the repository itself. L2 does not stop it either, because the process may read its own spawn home and `/proc/self`, so it does not change the level's class (§2.4.1). Codex at `none` has no read, and Gemini at `none` has no workspace directory. The UI says so and recommends scoped API keys. | Path confinement live checks (AG-23, AG-25) for the read levels, except Gemini's symlinked GEMINI.md, which stays open until a Gemini pin stops following it (A-K-05 (6) records the behaviour per pin); never for shell levels |
| R-2 | AG-04's startup window: a same-uid process can read a starting worker's env and can kill the worker to reopen it. | L2 for every spawn: AG-14, AG-15, AG-16 together. Never under `off`, nor for class U on hosts without L2. |
| R-3 | The worker process, including in-process plugin code (D-005), holds the DSN. Until AG-19, and on native installs always, such code can also read the master key file and decrypt every secret, whatever the worker's entrypoint imports (§2.5.2). From AG-17 the worker also holds the bot tokens of its squad peers while a squad route has returned them (§2.5.2), as every worker can decrypt any agent's token today (C10). Every worker also holds the runtime-access signing secret (`RUNTIME_LOCAL_UI_TOKEN`) in its env (`manager.py:12901-12910`), because its own runtime API verifies request tokens with it (`koda/services/runtime/api.py:87-101`). The resolver reads the agent's own secret from its snapshot first (`runtime_access.py:66-71`), then the global `cp_secret_values` row (`:73-79`), then `<AGENT>_RUNTIME_LOCAL_UI_TOKEN` (`:81-85`), then the global env value (`:86-88`). So only the agent's own secret, which travels in its snapshot, gives an agent its own runtime-access secret; the `<AGENT>_` variable does so only while no global secret row exists. Otherwise every agent shares it, and such code can mint `mutate` and sensitive runtime tokens for any agent. | M1-E2-T5, M1-E4-T4; AG-19 for the key, in compose only (never on native installs). The shared runtime-access secret is not closed by this ADR; the agent's own secret limits it to that agent |
| R-4 | Worker-asserted resolutions. A DSN holder can reset `uses` on a real grant, replaying a call a human approved with the same fingerprint. Any code holding the worker's broker token, such as in-process plugin code, can relay a Telegram decision the control plane cannot verify, and can send that agent's chat settings changes (`POST /internal/agents/settings-change`) within the closed patch list; the gate still refuses any capability they add. Until AG-17 and AG-19 ship, and on native installs always, a worker can also compute a grant MAC and insert a grant directly. | M1-E2-T5 (no write on the approval tables, once M3-E3-T2 moves those writes to `app`); M1-E4-T4 (plugins); AG-17 + AG-19 (MAC forgery), in compose only (never on native installs) |
| R-5 | Snapshot rollback across an `app` restart by a DSN holder; until AG-17 and AG-19 ship, and on native installs always, a worker can also forge a snapshot MAC. | M1-E2-T5 (no write on `cp_agent_config_versions` or `cp_agent_definitions`, after AG-17b and AG-22a); AG-17 + AG-19, in compose only (never on native installs, §2.5.2) |
| R-6 | UDP egress, the provider base-URL port to any host, and internal services on 443, 53 or that port, are reachable from class M under `strict`. | Not closed by this ADR; documented in `docs/security/threat-model.md` (AG-31) |
| R-7 | Instruction files and transcripts steer later turns. Gemini 0.68.0, at `read_only` and above, loads the workspace-root GEMINI.md at startup and appends the GEMINI.md files between a file a read tool reads and the workspace root (§2.3.2), whatever OQ-3(b) decides; nothing above the workspace loads, and at `none` nothing loads. For Claude and Codex this applies only if OQ-3(b) is declined. Claude's session transcript (`projects/koda/<session>.jsonl`) is copied into the spawn home for `--resume` and copied back to the agent's session store after the run (§2.3.5). A Claude turn at `workspace_exec` can rewrite its own session's copy through Bash, and the next resumed turn of the same agent and session loads the edit, including a turn at a lower level. It cannot reach the agent's other sessions: the store is granted to no CLI, and the copy-out follows no link, accepts only a regular single-link file, and names the destination from a bare-UUID session id and Koda's fixed project directory (§2.3.5). This is steering only: the resumed turn keeps the tools of its own level, because `--tools` is passed on every spawn. | Gemini: a version with a switch, recorded by the contract (A-K-05 (6) notices a change). Claude transcripts: accepted. Closing it would need the transcript outside the domain that Bash shares with Claude (§3) |
| R-8 | Under `off`, every class M process (Codex `workspace_write` and `workspace_exec`, Claude and Gemini `workspace_exec`, governed shell and git) can read every file the worker's uid can read: on native installs the master key, `bootstrap.txt` and every agent's home, sessions and workspace. Codex writes stay inside its writable roots; the others can write wherever the uid can, including the home of a concurrent spawn of any agent (§2.3.5). When Koda runs as root, that includes host-level CLI configuration that affects every agent (`/etc/claude-code`, `/etc/codex`; Gemini's system settings are pinned away, §2.3.5). Codex `read_only` is clamped to `none` for this reason. | L2 (`strict` or `filesystem`); never under `off` |
| R-9 | A worker started directly with `python -m koda --agent-id`, without the supervisor (kept working by Q-19), runs outside the boundary, like `off`: it builds its runtime snapshot in-process and so decrypts provider keys (C17), and no MAC is verified, because only the supervisor verifies. It is a single-process development mode. From AG-21 it never publishes, and a never-published agent fails with `agent_not_published`. It logs `unsupervised_start_outside_boundary` at start. Today every worker imports `koda/control_plane/bootstrap.py`, supervised or not (`koda/__main__.py:53-57`; the supervisor sets `AGENT_ID`, `supervisor.py:536`), and that module imports the manager and with it `decrypt_secret` (`bootstrap.py:9`; `manager.py:157`). Any other `koda.control_plane` submodule imports them too, through the package `__init__` (`koda/control_plane/__init__.py:3-5`). AG-17 takes them off the supervised path: `__main__` imports `bootstrap` only when `_KODA_RUNTIME_BOOTSTRAPPED` is unset, the supervised branch's pool reset (`bootstrap.py:28-45`) is deleted, because a supervised worker is a new process in which the pools it clears do not exist yet (revision 7), and the package `__init__` stops importing `bootstrap`, `manager` and `supervisor` (§5.1.4 (a)). After AG-19 this mode works only where the key file is readable (a native install or a development container), not in `runtime-kernel`. It draws its own incarnation and holds no per-worker kernel token, which the kernel mints only for a worker it spawns (AG-15), and no broker token, which the supervisor mints (AG-17), so from those items every call that needs one is refused in this mode with `unsupervised_start_no_token:<route>` (OQ-10). | Not closed. Making the direct start fail would remove functionality that Q-19 keeps, which needs §13 sign-off (§9.1); what the mode loses from AG-15 and AG-17 is OQ-10 |
| R-10 | A process the model starts can outlive its spawn in five cases (§2.3.5; revision 9 numbers them). (1) **`subreaper`, after its helper is killed first**: by the model's own code where Landlock cannot scope signals (ABI < 6, before kernel 6.12, or `off`), or by the OOM killer or an operator. Where AG-15b runs, the kernel, to which the dead helper's orphans are then reparented, ends them within a second, so they survive only if the kernel stops first; in compose the kernel is PID 1, which a command's `kill(-1, SIGKILL)` does not reach (`[V]`, §1.2), and its exit ends the container and every process in it (C11). Below ABI 6 that `kill(-1, SIGKILL)` also ends every other agent's same-uid worker, helpers and CLIs, interrupting their turns within the recovery limit (R-12). (2) **A native host below 6.12 outside `pid_namespace`** (`cgroup` or `subreaper`): there the kernel is not PID 1, so one `kill(-1, SIGKILL)` from a confined command also kills the worker and the runtime kernel (`[V]`, §1.2), and, as the same uid, every other agent's worker and spawns and, on a native host, the control plane (`app`) and the operator's own session (R-12); no worker, sweep or AG-15b is left to end the survivors. In `cgroup` they stay in their spawn cgroup until the kernel starts again and kills every spawn cgroup (§2.3.5); in `subreaper` they run until they exit or the host restarts. Ubuntu 24.04, whose 6.8 kernel has ABI 4 and whose AppArmor policy restricts unprivileged user namespaces, is expected to be such a host (`[I]`). (3) **`cgroup` under `off`**: a process can move itself out of the spawn's cgroup, because the uid owns the delegated subtree (R-8). (4) **`lifetime` `none`, reason `no_helper`** (macOS, `off` without the binary, and the unsupervised start of R-9 on such a host): only the CLI's process group is signalled, and every command the model ran in its own session, and anything it started with `setsid`, outlives the spawn and the worker; AG-15b is off in this mode. (5) **`lifetime` `none`, reason `lifetime_not_approved`**: while OQ-8 is pending, or if it is declined, the helper runs without `--lifeline` and execs the command (AG-08), so every descendant of a command, governed shell and `shell_bg` included, outlives it and the worker restart, as today. A survivor keeps the spawn's grants (the workspace, the task tmp, the TCP ports) until it exits or its container or host restarts. Koda's own stop of a stuck helper never orphans its descendants (§2.3.5, "Stopping a stuck helper"). Compose is expected to report `subreaper` on kernels below 6.12 (`[I]`, C11, §5.4); the confinement status and health show the mode and the case (§2.4.3). | (1): AG-15b, required for the release where AG-08 records `subreaper` below ABI 6, which compose is expected to, OQ-8 is answered (a) and OQ-9 (b) and (c) are approved (§6); otherwise open in compose; with it only a kernel that stops first remains, and at ABI ≥ 6 (where AG-15b is dropped) only the OOM killer and an operator. (2): ABI ≥ 6 or `pid_namespace` on the host. (3): L2. (4): not closed. (5): OQ-8 (a) or (b). `pid_namespace` or `cgroup` on the host avoids (1). In compose either other mode needs a container change that §2.4.3 rules out (§3) |
| R-11 | **Operator commands before AG-15** (revision 9). `/shell`, `/pip` and `/npm`, operator-authored scheduled shell jobs and the operator terminal of a runtime environment run as today until AG-15, unconfined in `runtime-kernel` (which mounts `koda-state` until AG-19), and so does `/git`, hardened from AG-03 once OQ-9 (a) is approved. They run in a work dir the model can write, so one of them can run code the model planted there: a Makefile target, an npm lifecycle script, a Python build hook. From AG-14 a confined model can therefore leave code that runs unconfined when an operator later types such a command, and when an operator-authored scheduled job fires, which needs no operator step: cron fires it, and so does the model's own `job_run_now` (`tool_dispatcher.py:1311-1323`). With OQ-11 (a), a shell job whose command or work dir the **model** wrote is governed, not an operator command (§2.9.1), so it does not use this path: S9 refuses it from AG-14 where the class M layers are missing. If OQ-11 (a) is declined it does, and the model then chooses the code as well. Today the model runs unconfined anyway (C1 to C3), so this is no weaker than today. | AG-15 with OQ-9 (b) (operator commands, operator-authored jobs, and model-written jobs if OQ-11 (a) is declined); AG-14's S9 with OQ-11 (a) (a model-written job); never if OQ-9 (b) is declined (operator path) |
| R-11a | **The kernel's own git, tar and copy where the helper cannot confine them.** From AG-03 until AG-15 the kernel runs its own git and tar (worktree creation and removal, checkpoint `diff`/`reset`/`apply`/`tar`), hardened with OQ-9 (a) and unhardened, as today, if (a) is declined, and the copy of a `copy` environment in-process on every host. From AG-15 it runs them through `koda-sandbox-exec` under the strongest task class M profile the host enforces (filesystem-only below ABI 4; they are Koda's bookkeeping, not a governed call, so S9 does not refuse them), and in-process only where Landlock is absent (a host below kernel 5.13) or under `off` without the binary. In-process they run in the kernel process, on a workspace the model can write (`workspace.rs:68-97, 130-138, 182-189`; `checkpoints.rs:23-73, 97-104, 145-150, 238-242`). With OQ-9 (a), the §2.9.1 flags and the `git config --file … --no-includes --list` allowlist stop a planted fsmonitor, hook and external driver (`[V]`, §1.2), but the config check cannot cover a path a later git subcommand reads, tar bundle names are checked only for `..` and absolute paths (`checkpoints.rs:114-142`), and the copy relies on §2.3.5's no-follow rules (§7.9). | AG-15 (the helper) where the host supports L2; in-process otherwise, hardened with OQ-9 (a), else as today (no worse than revision 9, which never ran them through the helper) |
| R-12 | **A confined command's `kill(-1, SIGKILL)` reaches every same-uid process below ABI 6** (revision 10). The signal-scoping layer needs Landlock ABI 6 (kernel 6.12); below it, or under `off`, a confined command can run `kill(-1, SIGKILL)`, which ends every process of the running uid but PID 1 and the caller (`[V]`, §1.2). In `runtime-kernel` (C11) that is every agent's workers, helpers and CLIs (the kernel is PID 1 and survives); on a native install, where `app`, the kernel and the workers share the operator's uid (R-3), it is also the control plane and the operator's own session. Each worker restart it causes counts one recovery for every task that worker was running, so a shell-level turn that runs it, repeated, pushes other agents' in-flight tasks to the DLQ after `QUEUE_MAX_RECOVERY_ATTEMPTS` (3, `queue_manager.py:8955-8975`). No weaker than today, where the model runs unconfined. | ABI ≥ 6 (kernel 6.12) signal scoping, or `pid_namespace` on the host (where `kill(-1)` reaches only the namespace); never below that outside `pid_namespace`. Recorded in the notice and tested with two agents (§5.4) |
| R-13 | **Two installs on one native host that share roots** (revision 11). `<bid>` is the agent id (`config.py:18-19`), not an install id, and the kernel has none (`isolation.rs:136-146`; its compose env sets no `AGENT_ID`, `docker-compose.yml:244-247`). So two runtime kernels on one native host that share `KODA_CGROUP_ROOT` (default `/sys/fs/cgroup/koda`) with a writable, delegated subtree (two installs, or a development kernel beside an installed one) share the `ws_*` names, and a starting kernel kills the other's spawn cgroups (§2.3.5, "At start"); and two installs under one OS user share `~/.koda-runtime/<bid>` for every agent id they have in common, so one install's worker sweep stops the other's live helpers and removes their homes (§2.3.5, "The sweep"). Two such installs already share more by default: the state root `~/.koda-state` with its master key (`config.py:205-206`) and the kernel root `/tmp/koda-runtime/default/kernel` (`server.rs:85-87`). Compose is not affected: its cgroup mount is read-only (C11), and each project has its own volumes. | Not closed. A second install or development kernel on one host sets its own `STATE_ROOT_DIR`, `RUNTIME_EPHEMERAL_ROOT`, `RUNTIME_KERNEL_ROOT` and `KODA_CGROUP_ROOT`, as the notice and AG-31's operations docs say. No install id is introduced: the roots are the only per-install anchor, and two installs that share them are not separate today |
| R-14 | **Work dirs the model chooses outside Koda's roots, and the install directory** (revision 12). If OQ-12 (a) is declined, a work dir the model sets itself (`agent_set_workdir`, `job_create`, `job_update`) needs only to pass the predicate, so on a native install it can be any directory under HOME that is not a Koda root (`validate_work_dir` accepts each one below, `[V]`, §1.2): a read grant on `~/.ssh` or `~/.aws` gives the model the operator's keys, and a read-write grant on `~/.config/systemd/user` or `~/.local/bin` lets it plant code that runs unconfined later (invariant 3). If OQ-12 (b) is declined, a work dir in the Koda install directory gives the model the `.env` that Koda loads into every worker (`config.py:15`; invariant 5) and the code Koda runs. Today the model runs unconfined and reaches all of these anyway (C1 to C3). | OQ-12 (a) and (b) approved; otherwise not closed |

### 2.6 Audit

Events go to the existing sinks (`control_plane/audit.py:22`, `services/audit.py`). Governance events (2.6.1 and the approval events) are written in the transaction of the action they record, and the action fails if they cannot be written (a `required=True` path in `record_audit_event`). The control-plane store has no multi-statement transaction today (every call autocommits on its own connection, §2.2.3; `record_audit_event` too, `audit.py:44`). So AG-07 adds one transaction helper, `run_in_transaction(fn)`, to `koda/state/control_plane_store.py`, with its async half in `koda/state/primary.py` next to the one-statement helpers (`:323-388`). It takes one pooled connection, sets the schema as those helpers do, opens a transaction and awaits `fn(conn)`, an async callable that may read, lock, compute and write on that connection. It commits when `fn` returns and rolls back every statement when `fn` raises. Sync callers reach it through `run_coro_sync` (`primary.py:143`), as every store call does. This is the interactive form `koda/squads/threads.py:195` already uses with its own pool (`async with pool.acquire() as conn, conn.transaction():`, then `fetchrow`). Revision 6 defined the helper as a list of statements, which is what `with_connection` already replays (`control_plane_store.py:106-120`); that form cannot lock a row, check a MAC in Python, or compute a MAC over an id the database assigns. The publish gate's write, approval consume and approval resolution all use this one helper (§2.2.3, §2.9.2). The `required=True` path writes through the caller's transaction.

#### 2.6.1 Opt-in change: `agent.provider_builtin_tools_changed`

Emitted by `publish_agent` whenever the level or the capability ceiling differs from the baseline, and always by the AG-21 migration and the legacy import. For the migration, `from_level` and `from_capability` are `null`: the version had no governance block and ran with the provider's bypass mode, although the gate now reads it as `read_only` (§2.2.3 step 2).

```json
{
  "event_type": "agent.provider_builtin_tools_changed",
  "agent_id": "string",
  "version": 42,
  "previous_version": 41,
  "from_level": "read_only",
  "to_level": "workspace_exec",
  "from_capability": ["read"],
  "to_capability": ["read", "write", "exec", "egress"],
  "providers": ["claude", "codex"],
  "escalation": true,
  "actor": { "kind": "operator", "id": "7" },
  "confirmed": true,
  "snapshot_signature_id": "string",
  "reason": "publish | migration | import"
}
```

`actor` follows the `koda:actor` schema (§2.2.2).

#### 2.6.2 Other events

1. **Configuration changes:** actor plus before/after diff on `put_section`, `put_execution_policy`, `put_autonomy_policy`, `put_model_policy` (`manager.py:12115, 8652, 8597, 8583`) and on system-settings writes, including `CODEX_BIN` and `GEMINI_BIN` (`manager.py:605, 617`).
2. **`provider.process_spawned`**, one per model-reachable process: agent, task, provider, process class, the spawn home id; CLI version and contract id; requested and effective level with clamp reasons; capability; confinement mode, ABI, the enforced layers and the lifetime mode (§2.3.5); the resolved L2 profile (workspace ro/rw, provider home, egress ports); for Codex the `IP` result for this profile and the SHA-256 of the `features list` output and of the Koda-written `config.toml`; for Gemini the SHA-256 and effective tool list of the Koda-written user settings, the result of the settings-warning check and of the A-K-05 probes, and the presence of the cwd stop file; for Claude and Codex the SHA-256 of each host-level configuration file present (O-10); the non-secret flag list (never prompt or query text); `CONTROL_PLANE_ACTIVE_VERSION` and the snapshot signature id.
3. **Spawn copies:** `provider.spawn_copy_refused` and `provider.credential_writeback_refused`, with the spawn home id, the file name and the reason, never the content (§2.3.5, §2.4.4); `provider.orphan_spawn_swept` per orphaned spawn a starting worker kills or removes; and `provider.spawn_helper_lost` when a helper exits on a signal before its command, with the lifetime mode, the launcher (the worker, or the kernel, which reports the helper's signal exit in the task's status or the command's response) and whether its descendants were ended (by the namespace in `pid_namespace`, by the launcher's `cgroup.kill` in `cgroup`, by AG-15b or not at all in `subreaper`, R-10) (§2.3.5).
4. **Provider-reported tool use:** built-in `tool_use` the CLIs report (`claude_runner.py:585-595, 905-906`; Codex JSON events) stored with `source=provider_reported`, arguments redacted and truncated.
5. **Secret broker:** one row per resolution.
6. **Approvals:** `approval.requested`, `approval.resolved` (resolver kind and id, decision, scope, fingerprint; never parameter values), `approval.grant_consumed`, `approval.expired`; and `security.approval_grant_mac_invalid` (alerting) for each candidate grant whose MAC fails at consume, with its id, agent and fingerprint (§2.9.2).
7. **Migration, restore and mode changes:** actor `system:migration` or the operator; `security.snapshot_rejected`; and AG-05's `security.master_key_fingerprint_recorded` (implemented, `crypto.py:336-341`).

### 2.7 Operator UX

Follows `apps/web/AGENTS.md` and `apps/web/CLAUDE.md`. Copy is PT-BR; existing PT-BR text is not translated.

- **Recursos tab**, next to the provider and model choice: a segmented control **"Ferramentas nativas do provedor"** with *Nenhuma / Somente leitura (padrão) / Escrita no workspace / Execução no workspace*. It replaces raw-JSON editing of this key.
- **Effective level per provider** from the resolver: "não se aplica" for HTTP providers, each clamp with its reason, the capability, and the confinement status. For Codex: "O Codex não tem modo somente leitura: em Nenhuma e Somente leitura ele responde sem ferramentas (sem shell e sem leitura de arquivos). Com o confinamento ativo, só Execução no workspace dá ferramentas ao Codex; sem confinamento, Escrita no workspace também."
- **Escalation modal** (§2.8.4 text) whenever the publish diff contains an escalation, including a provider added to the model policy. Only after confirmation does the client send `confirm_escalation=true`. The agent list shows a badge for agents above `[read]`.
- **Pending chat changes:** a model-policy change made in the chat and refused for escalation shows in the agent's draft as "Mudança pedida pelo chat, aguardando confirmação".
- **Execution detail** per turn: effective level, clamps, capability, confinement (from the spawn event) and provider-reported tool uses.
- **System health:** the mode, the ABI, the enforced layers, the spawn lifetime mode (with R-10 when it is `subreaper` or `none`), each CLI's pin match, the Codex `IP` results per profile, the Codex feature review, the Codex models proven at `none` (and any configured model shown as `codex_none_unproven`), the Gemini settings load, per-key and startup-context probes, and per agent `governance_migration_failed` or `snapshot_unsigned` when present. Under `off`, a red banner: "Confinamento desligado: comandos e ferramentas de escrita podem ler qualquer arquivo deste usuário do sistema, incluindo a chave mestra e os dados de outros agentes."
- **Rule simulator:** the existing evaluate endpoint, backed by the runtime engine (`apps/web/src/lib/control-plane.ts:1630` has no consumer today).
- **Approvals panel:** lists the agent's pending requests from Postgres (§2.9.2) with the verbs allow once, allow scope, deny with a reason.

### 2.8 Defaults, migration and rollback (MISSION §13)

#### 2.8.1 Defaults and existing agents (AG-21, AG-30)

- New agents get `read_only`.
- `koda policy audit` (AG-09, read-only) runs before the upgrade and is attached to the release. It has three sections: (1) decision diffs once rules are delivered (legacy matcher versus the engine, ADR-039); (2) per agent on a CLI provider, the built-in tool use seen in traces (provider-reported Write, Edit, Bash; Codex commands); (3) newly live governed calls, meaning every agent and rule that would let `shell_execute`, `shell_bg`, `git_*` or supervised writes run once M3-E3-T1 lands. Section 3 is M3-E3-T1's approval package.
- **Migration (AG-21).** A version without a governance block already runs at `read_only` (§2.2.3 step 2), so the migration is a record, not a precondition. At control-plane start, an idempotent migration gives every published agent whose last published version has no governance block a version N+1 built **from that published snapshot** (never `build_draft_snapshot`, `manager.py:12461`), with `level=read_only`, the ceiling of its providers at `read_only`, actor `system:migration`, no draft check, and one audit event; it is signed once AG-11 is present. The field is added to the draft only if the draft lacks it. No publish waits for it; an agent it fails for shows `governance_migration_failed:<agent>` in health and is retried at the next control-plane start.
- **Banner (AG-30).** The banner (§2.8.4) lists the agents that previously ran unrestricted; for Codex agents it says that Codex now answers without tools until the operator chooses Execução no workspace. "Restaurar execução no workspace" goes through the confirmed publish; it cannot restore bypass, which no longer exists.

#### 2.8.2 Settings

- **No longer read:** `CODEX_SANDBOX` and `CODEX_APPROVAL_POLICY` (removed from `config.py`, `startup_validator.py` and the system-settings fields). Their stored values are **not cleared**: they are listed in the release note, and AG-32 deletes them one release later (OQ-7). Startup is not refused because of them. `AGENT_EXECUTION_POLICY_JSON` is removed; AG-09 reports any value found.
- **Changed (AG-02, implemented):** `CODEX_BIN` and `GEMINI_BIN` are each one executable path. A command line reports `unavailable:unverified_cli_version`; a missing binary `unavailable:cli_not_installed`.
- **Changed defaults (AG-12):** native `RUNTIME_EPHEMERAL_ROOT` moves from the system temp dir to `~/.koda-runtime/<bid>`; compose `RUNTIME_KERNEL_ROOT` moves from `/tmp/koda-runtime/default/kernel` to `/var/lib/koda/runtime/kernel`. Both hold only ephemeral task files; Claude sessions kept for `--resume` start fresh after the move.
- **New:** `RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT`, `RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT_EGRESS_PORTS`, `WORKER_STATE_ROOT` and `AGENT_WORKSPACE_ROOT` (default `/var/lib/koda/runtime/workspaces` in compose and `~/koda-workspaces`, 0700, on native installs, which AG-12's predicate allows because it lies under HOME without containing it; each agent gets only its own `<AGENT_WORKSPACE_ROOT>/<agent>`, revision 12), each in `config.py`, `.env.example` and the docs.

#### 2.8.3 Installs, work dirs and rollback

- **Existing installs (AG-18, AG-19):** the release compose and CHECKSUMS are updated. On first start, `app` copies the worker subpaths from `koda-state` into `koda-worker-state`, verifies checksums and writes a marker (copy, not move); the legacy copies go one release later (AG-32, ADR-018). The key is not touched. The installer preflight runs `koda-sandbox-exec --self-test`; on ABI < 4 it requires the operator to choose `filesystem`, interactively or by flag, and records the choice.
- **Work dirs:** the default becomes `<AGENT_WORKSPACE_ROOT>/<agent_id>`. Files in the old shared HOME stay; the notice (§2.8.4) says so and names the work dirs AG-12's predicate refuses.

| Rollback scope | How |
|---|---|
| Per agent | Publish a higher level (confirmed, audited). |
| Per install | `RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT=filesystem`. |
| Per release | The previous images still find `koda-state` with the key, the legacy worker subpaths, the old login homes and the stored `CODEX_*` values. Rolling back **restores the previous ungoverned provider modes** (Claude `bypassPermissions`, Codex `danger-full-access` or the operator's stored value, Gemini `yolo`) **and the previous volume layout**, with the key mounted in `runtime-kernel` again. Changes after the upgrade to skill locks, gateway config, room photos and CLI logins are not carried back (documented). Old code ignores the new field, the governance block, signatures and the approval tables; every migration in this ADR is additive until AG-32. |

#### 2.8.4 Operator migration notice

**Release note and `docs/operations/upgrade.md` section (English):**

> ### Provider built-in tools now follow Koda policy (ADR-002)
>
> Claude Code, Codex and Gemini CLI no longer run with their own approvals turned off. Until this release Claude ran with `bypassPermissions`, Codex with `danger-full-access`, and Gemini in `yolo` mode, so an agent's provider could edit files and run commands without any Koda approval.
>
> **What changes when you upgrade**
>
> - Every agent gets the new setting **Provider built-in tools**, set to **Read only**. On upgrade Koda publishes a new version of each agent from its last published version, so pending drafts are not published. The report `koda policy audit`, which you can run before upgrading, lists the agents that used the provider's own edit or shell tools.
> - At **Read only**, Claude and Gemini can read files in the agent's workspace and do nothing else. **Codex has no read-only mode: at None and Read only it answers without any tools (no shell, no file reads).** With confinement on (the default), Codex gets tools only at **Workspace exec**; with confinement off, also at **Workspace write**. Below Workspace exec, Koda uses only the Codex models it has verified for the installed Codex version; system health lists any configured model that is not verified.
> - Edits and commands through Koda's own tools (files, shell, git) still ask for approval per call, and work for agents whose policy allows them once M3-E3-T1 is approved.
> - Raising an agent to **Workspace write** or **Workspace exec**, or adding a provider that gains capabilities at the agent's level (for example Codex on an agent at Workspace write), needs a confirmation in the dashboard.
> - Chat settings changes are now published right away. This covers the commands (`/provider`, `/model`, the feature-model, voice and voice-model commands), the buttons of the Telegram settings menu, and plain messages that ask for such a change (for example "mude o provedor para codex"). Each one publishes only its own change and no other pending dashboard edit. A change that would add capabilities is refused in the chat and waits in the dashboard for confirmation; the conversation keeps its current provider and model.
> - **Each published chat change restarts the agent.** A reply in progress gets up to 15 seconds to finish and is then stopped. Unfinished and queued messages run again after the restart, under the new settings. Each restart counts against the queue's recovery limit (`QUEUE_MAX_RECOVERY_ATTEMPTS`, default 3): a message interrupted by more restarts than that is moved to the dead-letter queue instead of running again. Once approvals move to the database (M3-E3-T2), pending approvals expire on restart and must be requested again; until then they are restored as before. Voice changes already worked this way.
> - **Model, provider and voice changes saved in the dashboard take effect when you publish.** Until this release, a saved draft change to these settings could reach the running agent as soon as someone opened a settings command or menu in the chat.
> - Provider CLIs no longer load hooks, MCP servers, plugins, extensions, skills or rules from the workspace or from their home directory, and no longer load CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md (subject to operator approval, OQ-3). Gemini also ignores the host's `/etc/gemini-cli` system settings and any `.gemini/.env` file in the directories above its working directory, including `~/.gemini/.env` on native installs. Put Gemini keys in the dashboard's provider connection instead. Gemini no longer loads GEMINI.md files from the directories above the workspace. At **Read only** and above it still loads the GEMINI.md at the workspace root when it starts, and adds the GEMINI.md files between a file it reads and the workspace root; Gemini 0.68.0 has no setting to turn this off. At **None** Gemini sees no workspace content at all. A GEMINI.md in the workspace that is a symbolic link is followed, so do not let untrusted content put links into a Gemini agent's workspace, and prefer scoped API keys.
> - Processes an agent starts through a provider's shell, or with Koda's shell or background shell, stop when that provider run or command ends, and all of them stop when the agent restarts (subject to operator approval, OQ-8). A server an agent starts in the background therefore does not outlive its turn. The Docker deployment is expected to use the weakest of Koda's mechanisms for this, as are hosts where Koda can use neither a PID namespace nor a cgroup (system health shows which). There, on Linux kernels before 6.12, a process that first kills Koda's launcher keeps running, with the permissions of the run that started it; in the Docker deployment Koda's runtime kernel stops it about a second later (where OQ-8 (a) and OQ-9 (b) and (c) are approved; otherwise it keeps running until it exits or the container restarts). On a native install on such a kernel where Koda cannot use a PID namespace (for example Ubuntu 24.04, which restricts them), such a process can also stop Koda's runtime kernel, and then keeps running until the runtime kernel starts again or, where Koda uses its weakest mechanism, until it exits or the host restarts; system health shows this case. On these same kernels (before 6.12), a shell-level turn of one agent that runs `kill -9 -1` also stops every other agent's worker and running turn, and, repeated, moves their queued messages to the dead-letter queue; on a native install it can also stop Koda's control plane and your own session. This is no different from today, where agent commands run without confinement.
> - Commands you type in Telegram (`/shell`, `/git`, `/pip`, `/npm`), scheduled shell commands **that you create** and the terminal of a runtime environment run in a directory the agent can write, so they can run code the agent left there. They therefore get the same confinement as an agent's commands (subject to operator approval, OQ-9). (A scheduled shell command whose command or directory the **agent** itself wrote, by creating it or by changing yours, is treated as one of the agent's own commands instead: it runs only where the agent's policy allows shell without approval, and when a run is refused, the chat says so. A job whose command and directory you set yourself stays yours, including after the agent only changes its schedule or runs it early. Subject to operator approval, OQ-11.) `/git`, and the git commands Koda runs for runtime environments, ignore your global and system git configuration and repository hooks, and refuse a repository whose configuration has keys outside Koda's list (the error names the key); a runtime environment is then not created for that repository. These commands can write only in their working directory and a temporary directory, cannot read your home directory, and connect only to the allowed ports (`RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT_EGRESS_PORTS`, default 443 and 53), so `pip install --user`, `npm install -g`, git over SSH and anything that writes outside the working directory stop working there; use a shell on the host for those. Processes they start in the background stop when the command ends, and an open runtime-environment terminal closes when the agent restarts, which every published chat change causes (OQ-8, OQ-9).
> - Each provider run gets a new, empty home directory. Only logins and Claude's conversation history carry over between runs. Logins you made in the dashboard keep working. A login refresh that happens during a run at **Workspace exec** is not saved, because the agent's own commands could have changed it. If a provider then asks you to log in again, use an API key for agents at that level (subject to operator approval, OQ-4).
> - Starting an agent directly with `python -m koda --agent-id`, without the control plane's supervisor, still works for published agents, but that process runs outside the confinement boundary (it decrypts provider keys itself). It also lacks the tokens that only the supervisor's start provides, so in it shell commands, runtime environments, MCP servers, squads, voice and image generation are refused (subject to operator approval, OQ-10). Use it only for development. In the Docker deployment it no longer works inside `runtime-kernel`, which no longer holds the master key.
> - `CODEX_SANDBOX` and `CODEX_APPROVAL_POLICY` are no longer read. Their stored values are kept for one release, so that a rollback uses them, and then deleted: {list}. `CODEX_BIN` and `GEMINI_BIN` must each be the path of one executable; a command line such as `npx -y @google/gemini-cli` reports `unavailable:unverified_cli_version`, and a missing binary reports `unavailable:cli_not_installed`.
> - On native installs, Koda's runtime files move from the system temp directory to `~/.koda-runtime`; Claude sessions start fresh once.
> - An agent's default working directory becomes its own directory under `AGENT_WORKSPACE_ROOT` (`/var/lib/koda/runtime/workspaces/<agent>` in Docker, `~/koda-workspaces/<agent>` on native installs) instead of the home directory; files already in the home directory stay there. Koda refuses as a working directory your home directory itself, a shared temporary directory (`/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/dev/shm`), a directory that contains one of them or Koda's runtime directories, anything inside Koda's state, login, artifact or provider-home directories, anything inside Koda's runtime directories other than the agent's own workspace and its task's runtime environment, and another agent's workspace. A project directory under your home keeps working. An agent can move its own working directory, or a scheduled job's, only into its workspace, a `PROJECT_DIRS` entry, its dashboard workspace or the directory you set with `/setdir`, and no working directory may be inside the Koda install directory (subject to operator approval, OQ-12). A second Koda install, or a development runtime kernel, on the same host needs its own `STATE_ROOT_DIR`, `RUNTIME_EPHEMERAL_ROOT`, `RUNTIME_KERNEL_ROOT` and `KODA_CGROUP_ROOT`: with shared ones, a starting runtime kernel stops the other's agent processes.
> - The control plane refuses to start when the master key does not match this database. If the original key is lost for good, follow `docs/operations/backup-restore.md` (the `.master.key.accept-undecryptable` file).
> - Model-directed processes run under Linux Landlock confinement (`RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT=strict`). On hosts with Landlock ABI below 4 (for example Debian 12 or Ubuntu 22.04 GA kernels) the installer asks you to choose `filesystem`. Below kernel 5.13, governed shell and git are refused and Codex answers without tools.
> - With `RUNTIME_CONFINEMENT=off` (development, macOS, native installs), any command an agent runs, and Codex at Workspace write or Workspace exec, can read every file your operating-system user can read, including the master key and other agents' data.
> - On a native install (without Docker), the agent's own worker process runs as your operating-system user next to the master key file and can read it in every confinement mode; only the Docker deployment keeps the key out of the agents' container.
>
> **How to restore an agent's previous behaviour:** Dashboard → Agents → the agent → Recursos → Ferramentas nativas do provedor → Execução no workspace → confirm → publish. Bypass modes no longer exist.
>
> **Rollback:** the previous images still work with your data. Rolling back restores the previous ungoverned provider modes (bypass, `danger-full-access`, `yolo`) and the previous volume layout, with the master key mounted in `runtime-kernel` again. Changes made after the upgrade to skill locks, gateway config, room photos and CLI logins are not carried back.

**Dashboard banner (PT-BR), shown to operators until dismissed:**

> **As ferramentas nativas dos provedores agora seguem a política do Koda.** Nesta atualização, {n} agentes que usavam Claude, Codex ou Gemini com acesso irrestrito passaram para **Somente leitura**. Edições e comandos feitos pelas ferramentas do próprio provedor ficam bloqueados até você escolher outro nível; o Codex responde sem ferramentas até Execução no workspace. As ferramentas do Koda (arquivos, shell e git) continuam pedindo aprovação por chamada. [Ver agentes afetados] [Revisar política]

Per affected agent: "{agente} · {provedor} · antes: irrestrito · agora: Somente leitura{, sem ferramentas no Codex} · [Restaurar execução no workspace]".

**Escalation modal (PT-BR):**

> **Confirmar elevação para {nível}?** Com esta mudança, sem aprovação por chamada, o modelo poderá: {editar arquivos do workspace} {executar comandos} {acessar a rede por HTTPS} {ler a credencial do provedor usada por esses comandos}. {Com o confinamento desligado, os comandos poderão ler qualquer arquivo deste usuário do sistema, inclusive a chave mestra.} {Com o confinamento ativo, o Codex só tem ferramentas em Execução no workspace; neste nível ele responde sem ferramentas.} A mudança fica registrada com o seu usuário. [Cancelar] [Confirmar e publicar]

Only the capabilities the escalation adds are listed; the `off` sentence appears only under `off`, and the Codex sentence only when Codex is among the agent's providers, the level is below `workspace_exec` and L2 is on.

### 2.9 Governed path: per-call authorisation (D-011) and cross-process approvals (D-012)

M3-E3-T1 and M3-E3-T2 implement this section. The flow is ADR-009's: decision, then request, then a fingerprinted grant, then execute.

#### 2.9.1 D-011: `ExecutionAuthorization`

- **Object.** A frozen dataclass in `koda/utils/approval.py`: `agent_id`, `task_id`, `tool_id`, `command_digest` (SHA-256 of the exact command string, or of the git argument string), `work_dir`, `basis` (`policy_allow | human_grant | operator_command | scheduled_job`), `rule_id`, `grant_id`, `snapshot_version`, `nonce`, `expires_at` (mint time plus the call's timeout).
- **Minted** only by `authorize_call(evaluation, call, ctx, grant=None)`, called in `_execute_tool_traced` after the policy decision (`tool_dispatcher.py:732-770`):
  - `deny` → never;
  - `allow` → `policy_allow`, with the matched `rule_id`;
  - `require_approval` or `allow_with_preview` → only with a grant consumed through the control plane for this call (§2.9.2) → `human_grant`;
  - supervised mode: a write call (`_is_write_tool`) needs `human_grant` even when the rule allows it, which replaces the contextvar check at `tool_dispatcher.py:779-790`;
  - dry run and the S2 to S6 ceilings keep refusing writes (`tool_dispatcher.py:791-799`).
- **Passed explicitly**, never through a contextvar: `_execute_tool_traced` passes it as a keyword to the handler; only the shell, `shell_bg` and git handlers accept it and pass it to `run_shell_command(…, authorization=)`, `bg_process_manager.start(…, authorization=)` and `run_cli_command_detailed(…, authorization=)`.
- **Checked and consumed** by those runners: `agent_id` equals `AGENT_ID`, `tool_id` matches, the digest and the work dir equal the values they are about to run, it has not expired, and its nonce is unused (an in-process set). Anything else returns "Error: command execution not authorised." and logs `execution_authorization_refused`.
- **No ambient state.** `_execution_approved` and `check_execution_approved` are deleted. `asyncio` copies a context into every task it creates, so a contextvar set around `execute_tool` would leak into `shell_bg`'s task and any other task spawned meanwhile. The background task holds nothing it could reuse.
- **Human commands and scheduled jobs** mint their own: `with_approval` and `dispatch_approved_operation` mint `operator_command` for the typed command (`koda/handlers/commands.py:984, 1010`; `koda/handlers/packages.py:36, 62`), and an operator-authored scheduled shell job mints `scheduled_job` from the published job definition (`scheduled_job_runtime.py:299-305`).
- **A shell job the model wrote (revision 10; operator question OQ-11 from revision 11).** The model can author a `shell_command` job itself (`job_create` with `job_type=shell_command`, `tool_dispatcher.py:1199-1222`) and rewrite the command and work dir of any of its user's jobs (`job_update`, `:1073-1101`; `scheduled_jobs.py:1473, 1506-1507`); both tools have access level `write`, outside the secure default (`[V]`, §1.2). Such a job chooses its own command and work dir (`tool_dispatcher.py:1116`) and activates, or reactivates, with no operator step (`auto_activate_after_validation` defaults `True`, `:1124-1127`; `scheduled_jobs.py:1282, 1583-1586`). Its read-only classifier does not confine it: a command that calls a workspace-relative helper is classified `read` by `resolve_integration_action('shell', …)`, passes `is_read_only_shell_command`, and runs that helper, and `is_blocked_shell` and `GIT_META_CHARS` do not stop it (`[V]`, §1.2). So neither R-11's "an operator starts them" nor the carve-out below holds for it. Its class is the job's `command_author` (§7.9), which the caller records whenever the command or work dir is written: `model` from the `job_create` and `job_update` handlers, `operator` from `/cron`, the dashboard (`runtime/api.py:382-391`) and migrations, never from a tool parameter (today `job_update` takes `actor_type` from the model's params, `tool_dispatcher.py:1090`). A schedule change and the model's `job_run_now` (`:1311-1323`) keep the class, because neither chooses what runs. With OQ-11 (a) the dispatcher runs a `model` job as a **governed class M call**, never on the `scheduled_job` basis's `_execution_approved` pass (`scheduled_job_runtime.py:218-305`): from AG-14 S9 refuses it wherever the mode's layers are missing (a kernel spawn counts as missing them until AG-15, C17), from AG-15 it runs through the helper under the agent's class M profile, and at fire time it runs only on `policy_allow`. No human is present then to grant a `require_approval` or `allow_with_preview` call, so such a rule, like the secure default, refuses the run, which is recorded as blocked and reported in the chat. If OQ-11 (a) is declined, a `model` job runs on the operator path below (R-11). AG-14 owns the column and the refusal, AG-15 the helper path. A-O-01 (d).
- **Their class (revision 9, narrowed in revision 10).** The operator commands, **operator-authored** scheduled shell jobs and the operator terminal of a runtime environment (`koda/services/runtime/api.py:1749-1762` → `controller.py:1955-1985` → the kernel's `OpenTerminal`, `server.rs:1412`, which forks a shell at `:700`) run through the kernel (`shell_runner.py:38-47`) in a work dir the model can write: the agent's workspace, a task's environment, or a directory set with `/setdir` (`commands.py:369-391`). A human starts them, but they can run code the model planted there. They are class M (§2.4.1), and they are not governed agent calls, so no policy rule or S9 applies to them. Per window (OQ-9):
  - **Until AG-15** they run as today, through the kernel's `ExecuteCommand` and `OpenTerminal`, unconfined (R-11). S9's refusal between AG-14 and AG-15 does not cover them: it governs what the agent's policy runs, and refusing them would take away commands operators use today while adding nothing to their path.
  - **From AG-03** (OQ-9 (a)), `/git` no longer sends `git {args}` as a shell string. It builds the hardened git argv below, checks the repository's config the same way, and sends the argv as `ExecuteCommand`'s `argv` (`commands.rs:126-133`), so it stays on the kernel path with the other operator commands and gets the same hardening as every git process Koda spawns. The kernel's own git and tar (worktree creation and removal, `workspace.rs:26-50, 130-138, 175-189`; checkpoints' `diff`, `ls-files`, `reset`, `apply` and the `tar` bundle, `checkpoints.rs:22-73, 97-104, 144-150, 238-242`) run with the same flags, env and config check (`[V]` that they run a planted fsmonitor and `post-checkout` hook today, §1.2); from AG-15 they run through the helper too, under the task's class M profile (the workspace, the repository's common directory for a worktree, and for a restore that one checkpoint's patch and bundle, read-only), and where the helper cannot confine them the hardened in-process path is residual R-11a. A refused repository gets no runtime environment, and its task runs in its work dir, as after any failed environment setup (`queue_manager.py:7760-7761`).
  - **From AG-15** (OQ-9 (b)) they run through the helper under the class M profile the kernel computes (§2.4.2): their work dir as the workspace (by the L4 rule), a fresh per-command tmp, the egress ports, no HOME. With OQ-8 (OQ-9 (c)), their descendants end with the command, and a terminal's with the terminal. Because AG-15 binds `OpenTerminal` to the requesting worker's incarnation (§2.3.5), an open terminal closes when the agent's worker restarts, which every published chat change causes (OQ-9 (c), the notice). Where that profile cannot be enforced, they are refused, as governed shell is.
  - The worker states the basis (`operator_command` or `scheduled_job`) in the request, but the kernel's profile does not depend on it: an exemption keyed on a label the worker sets would serve any code holding the worker's kernel token (§3).
- **Governed git cannot execute repository config (AG-03, AG-12).** `git_status`, `git_diff` and `git_log` are read tools that run without a grant (C16), so a file planted in `.git/` must not turn them into exec:
  - every git process Koda spawns (the governed `git_*` tools through `cli_runner`, worker-side git in `queue_manager.py` and `runtime/controller.py`, the operator `/git` command, and the kernel's own git in `workspace.rs` and `checkpoints.rs`, which a Rust builder with the same list serves) runs with `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null`, `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, `-c core.fsmonitor=false -c core.hooksPath=/dev/null -c core.pager=cat -c gc.autoDetach=false -c maintenance.autoDetach=false` (on every git version: git is the host's or the image's, not pinned, and 2.43 accepts the last key, `[V]`, §1.2), and `--no-ext-diff --no-textconv` where the subcommand takes them. `gc.autoDetach=false` makes any auto-gc finish inside the command instead of daemonizing: the lifetime rule and AG-15b otherwise kill that detached gc while it holds `.git/gc.log.lock`, and a stale `gc.log.lock` then silently stops every later auto-gc in that repository (`[V]`, §1.2). Its cost (revision 11): without detach git writes no `gc.log`, so in a repository whose unreachable loose objects are too young to prune, every commit reruns the foreground gc (`[V]`, §1.2); the commit is recorded before the gc runs, the gc is bounded by the command's timeout, and D11-13 checks that such a commit still returns. For the model's own git inside a class M spawn, where no `-c` is injected, AG-03's env builder sets only `gc.autoDetach=false` and `maintenance.autoDetach=false`, through `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_n` and `GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_n`: the model already runs its own code there, so a planted hook or fsmonitor gives it nothing, and the repository's own hooks keep running for the agent's commits as today;
  - before it runs, Koda reads the repository's own config with `git config --file <gitdir>/config --no-includes --list` (which runs nothing) and refuses when a key is outside an allowlist (`core.repositoryformatversion`, `core.filemode`, `core.bare`, `core.logallrefupdates`, `core.ignorecase`, `core.precomposeunicode`, `core.autocrlf`, `core.eol`, `remote.*.url`, `remote.*.pushurl`, `remote.*.fetch`, `branch.*.remote`, `branch.*.merge`, `branch.*.rebase`, `user.name`, `user.email`, `init.defaultbranch`, `extensions.objectformat`); the error names the key, so `filter.*`, `diff.*.textconv`, `core.sshCommand`, `include*` and the like are refused;
  - governed file tools refuse writes to any path with a `.git` component; provider built-in writes under `.git/` are a live check below `workspace_exec` (§5.3), and a CLI that allows them stays class M.
- **The per-agent enable is the published execution policy; no new field.** The secure default denies `shell_execute`, `shell_bg` and `git_*` (C5), so governed shell and git stay off until the operator publishes a rule that allows or asks for them. Only an operator publish can carry such a rule (§2.2.3, step 1). M3-E3-T1's choice "enable for the listed agents, or a per-agent enable that defaults to off" therefore means publishing rules for the listed agents (from `koda policy audit` section 3) or not.

#### 2.9.2 D-012: one request and grant store, written only by the control plane

- **Tables** (registry migration, BIGINT ids, agent scope):
  - `approval_requests`: `id`, `agent_id`, `worker_incarnation`, `origin` (`agent_call | operator_command`), `task_id`, `session_id`, `chat_id`, `tool_id`, `call_fingerprint`, `scope_fingerprint`, `description`, `preview_text`, `risk_class`, `status` (`pending | approved_once | approved_scope | denied | expired`), `created_at`, `expires_at`, `resolved_at`, `resolved_by_kind`, `resolved_by_id`, `resolution_reason`.
  - `approval_grants`: `id`, `request_id`, `agent_id`, `session_id`, `chat_id`, `kind` (`once | scope`), `fingerprint`, `max_uses`, `uses`, `expires_at`, `issued_by_kind`, `issued_by_id`, `mac`, `created_at`.
- **One writer.** Only `app` writes either table, through one module, `koda/control_plane/approvals.py`. The worker calls internal routes with its per-worker broker token (AG-17); `agent_id` always comes from the token, never the body:
  - `POST /internal/approvals/consume` with `tool_id`, `call_fingerprint`, `scope_fingerprint`, `session_id`, `chat_id` and an optional `request_id`. In one transaction through AG-07's helper (§2.6), the control plane locks the candidate grants with `SELECT … WHERE agent_id = <the token's agent> AND uses < max_uses AND expires_at > now() AND … ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 20 FOR UPDATE`, so an exhausted or expired grant never shadows a usable one (revision 8): a `once` grant for that `request_id` whose fingerprint is the call fingerprint; otherwise the `scope` grants whose fingerprint is the scope fingerprint and whose session and chat match (as today, `approval.py:1371-1389`). It verifies each row's MAC in Python, newest first, and takes the first that verifies (revision 9). Each row whose MAC fails is skipped and writes `security.approval_grant_mac_invalid` (alerting, §2.6.2), so a row inserted with the DSN and a bad MAC can neither shadow a valid grant nor go unnoticed. On success it increments `uses`, writes `approval.grant_consumed` on the same connection, and returns `{grant_id, kind}`. With no usable grant it returns `404 no_grant`, and the transaction writes only those MAC events. More than 20 forged rows newer than a valid grant hide it and cost the call a new request and card, never an execution; the events show them. The worker mints `human_grant` only from a success.
  - `POST /internal/approvals` creates a request (`expires_at = now + APPROVAL_TIMEOUT`, 300 s, `approval.py:80`), sends `NOTIFY approval_requested`, and returns its id; the worker then shows the Telegram card.
  - `POST /internal/approvals/{id}/telegram-decision` relays a Telegram button press (Telegram user id, decision, scope).
- **Order for every call that needs a grant:** consume first (an existing scope grant covers the call with no request and no card); only on `no_grant`, create a request and show the card; after the resolution, consume with the request id.
- **Resolution** has one function, `resolve_approval_request(request_id, decision, scope, resolver)`, used by the dashboard route (operator session; it replaces `approval_broker`'s in-memory resolve) and by the Telegram relay. In one transaction (the same helper) it writes the status, takes the grant's id from its sequence (`nextval`), computes `mac = HMAC(signing key, canonical(id, request_id, agent_id, session_id, chat_id, kind, fingerprint, max_uses, expires_at, issued_by))` over that id with the ADR-025 signing key (M1-E6-T3), inserts the grant with both, writes `approval.resolved`, and sends `NOTIFY approval_resolved` with the request id.
- **Waiting.** The worker waits on `approval_resolved` through the one reconnecting LISTEN implementation (ADR-011, `koda/agents/listen_notify.py`), with a 5 s poll of the request status as the fallback, until `expires_at`; then it consumes.
- **Single use and expiry.** Allow once is `max_uses = 1`, and the consume transaction makes concurrent uses impossible. Scope grants keep the existing `ApprovalScope` TTL and `max_uses` (`approval.py:1413-1414`).
- **Restart.** At each worker spawn the supervisor marks that agent's `pending` requests from another incarnation `expired` (`approval.expired`, reason `worker_restart`). Nothing is restored, so D-163 disappears. Grants persist until they expire (ADR-009).
- **The boundary, stated exactly.**
  - The Telegram relay is a resolution the **worker asserts**. Telegram updates reach the worker, and a callback query carries nothing the control plane can verify; it checks only that the request belongs to the token's agent and is pending, and that the asserted Telegram user is on that agent's published allowed-user list. Any code that holds the worker's broker token can therefore approve that agent's own requests (R-4). The chat settings route (`POST /internal/agents/settings-change`, §2.2.3) is asserted the same way and checked the same way. Its reach is smaller: a patch from the closed list, which the publish gate refuses when it adds a capability.
  - What keeps a prompt-injected model out is that it cannot obtain the broker token: AG-03 keeps it out of every child env, AG-04 makes the worker non-dumpable, and L2 denies ptrace and `/proc/<worker>/environ` and `mem` (D12-14). Before AG-14 and AG-15, a same-uid process during the AG-04 startup window can read it (R-2).
  - The grant MAC stops a grant row inserted directly with the DSN only when the inserter cannot compute the signing key, which holds from AG-17 and AG-19 on, in compose; before then, and on native installs always, a worker can forge it (R-4). That is no weaker than today, where grants live in worker memory and JSON files.
  - Cross-agent use is refused at consume: every row and MAC carries `agent_id`, checked against the token.
- **Retired:** `_PENDING_OPS`, `_PENDING_AGENT_CMD_OPS` and `_APPROVAL_GRANTS` (`approval.py:78-79, 1588`), the restore path (`approval.py:86-153`), and `koda/state/pending_approvals.py` and `koda/state/approval_grants.py` with their JSON fallbacks. Existing file grants are not imported as live grants: they were written by workers and would become MAC-signed grants (§9.3, M2-E1-T2).

---

## 3. Alternatives considered

| Alternative | Why not |
|---|---|
| **Fewest moving parts.** A boolean in `autonomy_policy`, honoured only in `guarded` mode; the key stays mounted; network unconfined; dashboard approvals proxied to the worker. | Contradicts ADR-002(B) and ADR-009 (which rejects the proxy). Inert for general tasks and dependent on a query-text classifier. *Grafted:* confining worker-side git, the AST spawn guard, the work-dir invariant, supervised stepping in Koda's loop. |
| **Key relocation** to an app-only volume (copy, verify, delete). | Surgery on key material, and a downgrade trap (older releases created a new key when the file was missing). Making the existing volume app-only reaches the same boundary. |
| **Default `none`, with `strict` imposed silently.** | Silently removes reads from Claude and Gemini agents and governed shell on kernels older than 6.7. |
| **Codex `read_only` through L2 only** (inner `danger-full-access`, Koda maps the workspace read-only): revision 1, and option (ii) of S2-2. | Under L2 the shell gets TCP 443 and 53 to every host at `read_only` (HTTPS egress), and under `off` nothing confines it. |
| **Codex's own `--sandbox read-only` for `read_only`** (revision 2). | Cannot start inside Landlock, so under the default modes Codex could run no command and read nothing while the UI claimed reads; unconfined it reads every file the uid can read. |
| **A nested `koda-sandbox-exec` around Codex's shell** (a narrower Landlock domain with no TCP, through `$SHELL` or a wrapper). | Unproven that Codex 0.169.0 runs `exec_command` through a configurable shell; recorded as O-9 for a later pin. |
| **Refuse Codex entirely under `off` when the worker can read the key.** | Codex `read_only` already clamps to `none`, and the confirmed levels show R-8 in the modal; refusing would remove the only Codex modes native installs have. |
| **Gemini per-task system settings file** (revision 2). | Gemini 0.68.0 skips it unless it and every ancestor are root-owned, and ignores `admin.*` from any file (§1.2). |
| **Root-owned static Gemini settings in the image** (option B of R2-3). | Works only in the image, not on native installs, and still needs the workspace out of the cwd; one Koda-written user-settings file in the rebuilt home works everywhere. |
| **`--skip-trust`.** | Trust arrives after the first settings load and the 10 s cache, so the workspace layer and `.env` would load only on later reloads. Env trust with a Koda-owned cwd that holds only the stop file is the same on every load. |
| **`GEMINI_RESTRICTED_MODE=true` with folder trust off** (untrusted cwd, so no JIT GEMINI.md and no `.gemini/.env`). | Undocumented variable; forces `default` mode, so only `none` and `read_only` could use it, giving two trust modes per level. Revisit if OQ-3(b) for Gemini matters. |
| **Gate chat toggles by an engine-simulated widening of shell, git and write decisions** (SEC alternative in S2-4). | Needs a probe-call corpus and a simulate diff on every toggle. Publishing the last published snapshot plus the toggle's own change is smaller and also stops unrelated draft content. |
| **Keep the model policy live** (option (a) of R2-7: carve it out of the signed snapshot and keep S10 as the gate). | Two delivery channels for governance, and the chat command would take effect without any publish or audit. It remains only as the interim state between AG-21 and AG-21b. |
| **Chat commands keep publishing inside the worker** (the worker keeps the signing key and write access to `cp_agent_*`). | The worker could then sign any snapshot, so the MAC would protect nothing against a worker (R-5), and M1-E2-T5 could never deny those writes (R3-1). |
| **The `telegram_user` part of AG-21 waits for AG-17** (REV's first option in R3-1), with the model-policy switch moved into AG-21 (R3-2). | The gate and the migration would then wait for the broker, a high-risk item. Splitting the chat part into AG-21b keeps AG-21 independent and still lands the model-policy switch in the same merge as the chat publish, which is what R3-2 asks for. |
| **Apply chat changes without restarting the worker** (hot reload). | A second delivery channel next to the signed spec. The restart already happens for the voice toggle today, and tasks survive it (C17). |
| **Stop the Gemini `.env` search in the home** (`<gemini home>/.gemini/.env`, REV's option in R3-4). | It also works (verified). The cwd's stop file is chosen because the cwd is the first directory searched, so the search never looks above the cwd and does not rely on HOME being the cwd's parent. |
| **Pin every variable Gemini reads from `.env`.** | A denylist that misses each new variable a version adds (S3-1). The stop file makes the search read nothing. |
| **Keep the Gemini system settings path absent by the rebuild** (REV's wording in R3-4). | A path under `/dev/null` can never exist, so nothing has to keep it absent. |
| **Refuse the `system` publish inside the runtime-snapshot fallback** (SEC's recommendation in S3-4). | Deleting the fallback is smaller, because no caller needs it: the supervisor and bootstrap already publish a never-published agent first. The general rule, that a `system` publish of an agent with versions is refused, still covers the reconcile path. |
| **Disable `instant_interrupt`.** | It adds no tool, process or authority, and it does not change the model-visible input (§1.2). Keeping the default avoids changing Codex's interrupt behaviour without evidence. |
| **Close the Claude transcript residual** by checksumming transcripts between turns, or by replaying instead of resuming after a `workspace_exec` turn. | A checksum cannot tell Claude's own appends from an edit Bash makes during the same run. Replay after every `workspace_exec` turn costs a full replay to block steering that gives the model nothing beyond its own level (R-7). |
| **Serialise provider spawns per agent** (REV's alternative in R4-1). | An agent with ten queued tasks would run one CLI at a time, and a shared home would still be granted read-write to a `workspace_exec` shell that could plant files for the next spawn. Per-spawn homes cost one directory per spawn. |
| **Per-spawn homes under the task tmp** (REV's location in R4-1). | Governed shell, `shell_bg` and git get the task tmp read-write under L2, so a background shell of the same task could plant files in the next spawn's home. |
| **Keep Codex's developer instructions on argv** (today, `codex_runner.py:171-174`). | `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` stays readable by any process of the same uid, even under Landlock `[I]`, and the instructions carry a user's memory context (C18). A 0600 file in the spawn's own home is private to that spawn. |
| **Link the Claude session store into the spawn home** instead of copying one transcript in and out. | It would grant the CLI the whole store, so a `workspace_exec` Bash could rewrite every session of the agent rather than its own. Copying one named file, by the copy rules of §2.3.5 (no link is followed, the session id is a bare UUID, the project directory is Koda's), keeps the store out of every L2 grant and out of reach of a link planted in the home. |
| **A migration that blocks publishing** (`governance_migration_pending`, revision 4). | REV's simpler option in R4-2: a missing block already means `read_only`, so reading a block-less version that way removes the refusal, the "unrestricted" baseline and the per-agent retry, and the migration can never block a publish. |
| **Lock the agent's `cp_agent_definitions` row for the whole gate** (revision 5; REV's second option in R5-2). | The store has no multi-statement transaction, so the lock would be a statement on one connection while the rest of the gate ran on others (§2.2.3). The draft build writes that same row when the endpoint changes (`manager.py:12497-12513`), so the gate would wait on itself for up to `PRIMARY_STATE_BRIDGE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` (30 s, `koda/state/primary.py:30`) and fail. Making it work needs a connection-scoped transaction layer, the build outside the lock and AG-22a first. The compare-and-swap needs only the short write transaction, which the audit event needs anyway, and the unique constraint that exists. |
| **Keep Gemini's directory tree at `read_only` and above.** | The read tools list the workspace on demand; one setting at every level keeps the settings file the same apart from `tools.core` and `disableYoloMode`, and keeps `none` free of workspace content even if a later change passed the workspace there. |
| **Refuse a Gemini spawn when a workspace GEMINI.md is a symlink.** | A check before the spawn sees only the files present then; JIT reads subdirectories later, and a scan of the whole workspace before every spawn costs a tree walk. It would not close R-1, so the residual is recorded with the scoped-key advice instead. |
| **An import-closure assertion for the whole supervised worker** (SEC's §5.1.4 recommendation in S5-1). | It cannot pass while workers read through the manager at all: 20 worker modules import it, all but `agent_settings.py` lazily, and for reads that touch no secret (C10), and it imports `crypto` at module level (`manager.py:157`). Removing every such read is a refactor of 20 modules that buys no security, because importability is not the boundary (§2.5.2). Revision 6 keeps the import part where it holds by construction, the supervised env step (§5.1.4 (a)), and checks calls for the whole worker (§5.1.4 (c)). |
| **Make `bootstrap.py` import the manager lazily** (SEC's option (b) in S5-1). | The module would still hold both branches, so a check would have to trust the early return, and the package `__init__` would still import the manager. Skipping the import in the supervised branch, whose reset clears nothing in a new worker process (revision 7, R6-7), and emptying the package `__init__` make the supervised imports free of the manager by construction, and `sys.modules` proves it. Moving the reset into a new module (revision 6) is not needed. |
| **Read the published row directly for AG-22a's squad readers** (REV's second option in R5-6). | It is a live row read that the worker cannot verify, because only `app` holds the MAC key, so it would be an exception to §2.2.1. The read route returns only the closed field list, checks the squad, and verifies the MAC in `app`. |
| **Kill a worker's spawns with it by `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` set in each spawn** (revision 6's alternative). | The death signal follows the thread that forked the child, not the process, and it removes no home. The lifeline pipe gives the same effect for the whole process (§2.3.5). Init uses `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` only towards the helper, which is single-threaded. The sweep still removes the homes. |
| **Kill only the CLI's process group, at the end of a spawn and in the sweep** (revision 6). | Codex and Claude run each model command as the leader of its own session, so the commands and anything they start with `setsid` survive both (`[V]`, §1.2; S6-1, R6-1). |
| **A per-spawn cgroup only** (REV's recommendation in R6-1), **or a PID namespace only**. | Each can be missing where the other is present: a native host that restricts unprivileged user namespaces (an AppArmor rule, for example) can still delegate a cgroup, and a host without a delegated cgroup usually allows user namespaces. The namespace comes first because it holds even when the helper is SIGKILLed and needs no cleanup; a cgroup needs the worker or the sweep to kill it when the helper dies, and under `off` the uid can leave it. The subreaper (SEC's fallback) is the mechanism every Linux host has, and R-10 names what it leaves. Both reviewers' mechanisms are kept; this adds the order. |
| **Make compose provide a namespace or a cgroup** (a seccomp profile that allows the namespaces, a writable cgroup mount). | §2.4.3 rules out user-namespace and seccomp relaxations, and Docker mounts the cgroup filesystem read-write only for a privileged container (C11, `[I]`). Compose takes the `subreaper` mode, and R-10 says so. |
| **Ask the operator to accept the compose residual instead of AG-15b** (REV's other option in R7-4). Revision 7 kept "the runtime kernel kills every process it adopts" here as the next step. | The hosts §5.4 expects for compose run kernels below 6.12, so the default deployment would let any shell-level turn keep a process with its grants by killing one launcher. AG-15b closes that with one scan loop in a process that already reaps (the kernel is PID 1 in compose, C11, and the parent of every worker, `supervisor.py:5-6`). Revision 7's objections are answered: the kernel records every child it starts, through one spawn function, the `std` `.output()` and `.status()` sites included, and waits only on the pids it adopted, so it never takes an exit status tokio or `std` waits for (revision 9 lists every site); and from AG-14 to AG-16 every spawn runs under a helper that is itself a subreaper in every mode (revision 9), so an orphan reaches the kernel only when its helper has died, and an orphan in the kernel's subtree is exactly what a killed helper left behind, class U included. Revision 8 said this while the helper was a subreaper only in the third mode, where in `cgroup` mode AG-15b would have killed a live spawn's background server (R8-2, `[V]` §1.2). Under `lifetime` `none` AG-15b is off. It stays conditional on AG-08's record, because at ABI ≥ 6 the model cannot kill its helper. OQ-8 still asks the operator, because AG-15b ends processes, and offers this alternative as its answer (b). |
| **Route the operator `/git` through the worker-side git runner** (`_run_git` or `run_cli_command_detailed`; SEC's recommendation in S8-1). | That moves `/git` to the worker side, where from AG-14 it runs under worker-side confinement while `/shell`, `/pip` and `/npm` stay on the kernel path until AG-15: one operator command would change at a different time from the others. The same hardened argv sent as `ExecuteCommand`'s `argv` gives `/git` AG-03's hardening and config check, and keeps one window for every operator command (§2.9.1). |
| **Exempt operator commands from confinement** (class T, unconfined after AG-15), or confine them only when the worker labels a request as the agent's. | They run code the model can plant in their work dir (`[V]` for git's fsmonitor and hooks, §1.2), so a confined model could leave code for the operator's next command and escape L2 through it. And the kernel can tell them apart only by a label the worker sets, which any code holding the worker's kernel token could set (R-3). OQ-9 asks the operator; this is its declined branch. |
| **Require an operator to activate a model-written shell job** (SEC's alternative in S9-1), or refuse the model's `job_update` and `job_run_now` on operator-authored jobs (REV's alternative in R10-2), instead of OQ-11's governed call. | Each also changes what users' agents can do today (a model's job activates on its own, `auto_activate_after_validation` defaults `True`), so each would be an operator question as well, and an operator-activated job still runs unconfined before AG-15. OQ-11's governed call restricts only the code the model wrote, closes the escape in the same windows as governed shell, and keeps the model's edits working where the published policy allows shell; `job_run_now` does not choose the code, so R-11 covers it. Revision 10 said the governed call needed no operator decision; it does, which is why it is OQ-11. |
| **Keep the kernel's own git and tar hardened but in-process** (revision 9), rather than running them through the helper. | The flags and the config check stop a planted fsmonitor and hook (`[V]`, §1.2), but the check-then-run leaves the kernel process reading and writing a workspace the model controls, which contradicts invariant 3. Running them through `koda-sandbox-exec` under the task profile puts them behind L2 where the host supports it; where it does not, R-11a names the hardened in-process residual. |
| **Grant `<base>/.git` for a worktree environment** (a literal reading of revision 9), or grant the base repository's whole common directory unconditionally. | `<base>/.git` does not exist when the base is a repository subdirectory and is a file when it is a linked worktree (`[V]`, §1.2), so git in the new worktree would fail; and granting the common directory unconditionally would grant the config and hooks of a repository the operator uses outside Koda.
